\Pi_{\infty}^{\mathrm{cond}}\colon\textbf{{Sch}}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}\to\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})
is a hypercomplete integral cosheaf.

Proof.

By
[
Wol22
, Theorem 1.2]
, we have a natural equivalence

X_{\proet}^{\hyp}\simeq\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathrm{Gal}(X),\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Ani}}))\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}

hence second assertion is an immediate consequence of the first.
By
Proposition

3.38
, the third assertion is also an immediate consequence of the first.
Thus, we only need to prove the first assertion.

Using
Corollary

6.10
, it follows that for any integral hypercover
X_{\bullet}\to X
and
n\smallin\mathbf{N}
, the canonical map

\mathrm{Gal}(\cosk_{n-1}(X_{\bullet})_{n})\to\cosk_{n-1}(\mathrm{Gal}(X_{\bullet}))_{n}

is an equivalence.
Thus,
Propositions

6.9
and
6.13
imply that
\mathrm{Gal}(X_{\bullet})
is a hypercover of right fibrations of condensed categories.
Since sifted colimits are computed pointwise in the
\infty
-category
\Cond(\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})=\mathrm{Fun}^{\times}(\textbf{{Extr}}^{\op},\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})
, the claim follows by combining
Remarks

6.4
and
6.15
.
∎

We can also recover the schematic description of the over category
\mathrm{Gal}(X)_{/\bar{x}}
given in
[
BGH20
, Corollary 12.4.5]
:
5
5

5

The description of the under categories of
\mathrm{Gal}(X)
in terms of strict henselizations in
loc. cit.
is immediate from the definition.
The description of over categories in terms of strict normalizations is less obvious, so we decided to include an argument here.

6.17 Corollary
.

Let
X
be a qcqs scheme, let
\bar{x}\to X
be a geometric point, and let
X^{(\bar{x})}
denote the
strict normalization
of
X
at
\bar{x}
in the sense of
[
BGH20
, Notation 12.4.2]
.
Then the natural integral morphism
f\colon X^{(\bar{x})}\to X
induces an equivalence of condensed categories

\mathrm{Gal}(X^{(\bar{x})})\similarrightarrow\mathrm{Gal}(X)_{/\bar{x}}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Proof.

Since the morphsism
f
is integral, by
Proposition

6.9
the functor of condensed categories
\mathrm{Gal}(f)
is a right fibration.
Hence for
\bar{x}\colon{\mathbin{*}}\to\mathrm{Gal}(X^{(\bar{x})})\to\mathrm{Gal}(X)
, the induced functor

f_{/\bar{x}}\colon\mathrm{Gal}(X^{(\bar{x})})_{/\bar{x}}\to\mathrm{Gal}(X)_{/\bar{x}}

is an equivalence of condensed categories.
The condensed category
\mathrm{Gal}(X^{(\bar{x})})
already has a terminal object induced by the generic point of
X^{(\bar{x})}
, which is given by
\bar{x}\to X^{(\bar{x})}
, cf.
[
Mai25
, Theorem 2.4.21]
.
We conclude using that

\mathrm{Gal}(X^{(\bar{x})})\simeq\mathrm{Gal}(X^{(\bar{x})})_{/\bar{x}}\simeq\mathrm{Gal}(X)_{/\bar{x}}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}\qed

Finally, using some of the machinery developed in
[
Mar21
]
, we can also deduce integral basechange for proétale hypersheaves.
We do not need this in the rest of this article, but it might be of independent interest.

6.18 Proposition
.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

be a cartesian square of qcqs schemes where
f
is integral.
Then the induced square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{\prime})_{\proet}^{\hyp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\proet}^{\hyp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y^{\prime})_{\proet}^{\hyp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\proet}^{\hyp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{{\mathbin{*}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{{\mathbin{*}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{{\mathbin{*}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{{\mathbin{*}}}}

is horizontally left adjointable, i.e., the natural exchange transformation
p^{*}f_{{\mathbin{*}}}\to g_{*}q^{*}
is an equivalence.

Proof.

By
[
zbMATH07671238
, Corollary 1.2]
, this square is identified with the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathrm{Gal}(X^{\prime}),\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Ani}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathrm{Gal}(X),\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Ani}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathrm{Gal}(Y^{\prime}),\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Ani}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathrm{Gal}(Y),\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Ani}}))\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Gal}(q)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Gal}(g)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Gal}(f)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Gal}(p)_{*}}

Since
f
is integral,
Proposition

6.9
shows that
\mathrm{Gal}(f)
is a right fibration, and
Corollary

6.10
shows that the natural map
\mathrm{Gal}(X^{\prime})\to\mathrm{Gal}(X)\times_{\mathrm{Gal}(Y)}\mathrm{Gal}(Y^{\prime})
is an equivalence.
Because right fibrations of condensed
\infty
-categories are
proper
functors
[
Mar21
, Proposition 4.4.7]
, the the above square is horizontally left adjointable.
∎

6.2
Digression: strongly künnethable morphisms of schemes

We conclude this section by explaining at what level of generality the Künneth formula for étale
\infty
-topoi (equivalently,
Corollary

6.10
) holds.

6.19 Definition
.

We call a morphism of schemes
X\to Y

strongly künnethable
if for any morphism
Y^{\prime}\to Y
the induced map

(X\times_{Y}Y^{\prime})_{\et}\to X_{\et}\times_{Y_{\!\et}}Y^{\prime}_{\et}

is an equivalence.

6.20 Remark
.

Since all
\infty
-topoi involved in
Definition

6.19
are
1
-localic, being strongly künnethable is equivalent to the canonical geometric morphism

(X\times_{Y}Y^{\prime})_{\et,\leq 0}\to X_{\et,\leq 0}\times_{Y_{\!\et,\leq 0}}Y^{\prime}_{\et,\leq 0}

of
1
-topoi being an equivalence.

6.21 Proposition
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a morphism of finite presentation.
Then
f
is strongly künnethable if and only if it is quasi-finite.

Proof.

Let us first assume that
f
is quasi-finite.
Since open immersions are strongly künnethable by
[
HTT
,
Remark 6.3.5.8
]
, we may immediately reduce to the case where
X
,
Y
, and
Y^{\prime}
are affine.
Applying Zariski’s main theorem, we can factor
f
as an open immersion followed by a finite morphism.
Thus we may assume that
f
is finite.

We have to check that the induced map

(6.22)

(X\times_{Y}Y^{\prime})_{\et,\leq 0}\to X_{\et,\leq 0}\times_{Y_{\et,\leq 0}}Y^{\prime}_{\et,\leq 0}

is an equivalence.
By
Corollary

6.10
, it induces an equivalence of categories of points.
Furthermore it follows from the site-theoretic description of the fiber product of topoi
[
ILO14
, Exposé XI, §3]
that (
6.22
) is a coherent geometric morphism of coherent topoi.
Thus, the Makkai–Reyes conceptual completeness theorem
[
SAG
,
Theorem A.9.0.6
]
implies that this geometric morphism is an equivalence.

For the converse, assume that
f
is not quasi-finite.
Then at least one geometric fiber of
f
is not quasi-finite.
Since taking geometric fibers is compatible with taking étale
\infty
-topoi
[
HHW24a
, Proposition 2.3]
, we may reduce to the case where
Y=\mathrm{Spec}(k)
is the spectrum of a separably closed field
k
.
Furthermore, we may always modify
X
by quasi-finite maps to reduce to the case where
X
is integral of dimension at least
1
.
By Noether normalization, there exists a finite surjective map
h\colon X\to\mathbf{A}_{k}^{n}
.
Let
X_{\bullet}\to\mathbf{A}_{k}^{n}
denote the Čech nerve of
h
.
Now if
f
were strongly künnethable, then since the maps
X_{m}\to\mathrm{Spec}(k)
are the composite of a finite map
d_{0}\colon X_{m}\to X
and
f
, it would follow that also all maps
X_{m}\to\mathrm{Spec}(k)
would be strongly künnethable as well.
Thus for every
k
-scheme
Y^{\prime}
and every
m\geq 0
, the induced map

\mathrm{Gal}(X_{m}\times Y^{\prime})\to\mathrm{Gal}(X_{m})\times\mathrm{Gal}(Y^{\prime})

would be an equivalence.
But by integral descent (
Corollary

6.16
), after passing to the colimit over
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}
, this would imply that the canonical map

\mathrm{Gal}(\mathbf{A}_{k}^{n}\times Y^{\prime})\to\mathrm{Gal}(\mathbf{A}_{k}^{n})\times\mathrm{Gal}(Y^{\prime})

is an equivalence.

Thus we may assume that
X=\mathbf{A}_{k}^{n}
and therefore even that
X=\mathbf{A}_{k}^{1}
.
Now let
Z=\mathbf{A}_{k}^{1}
as well.
This would imply that the canonical map

\mathrm{Gal}(\mathbf{A}_{k}^{2})\to\mathrm{Gal}(\mathbf{A}_{k}^{1})\times\mathrm{Gal}(\mathbf{A}_{k}^{1})

is an equivalence.
In particular, it would induce an equivalence on underlying posets and thus an isomorphism of specialization posets

{(\mathbf{A}_{k}^{2})}_{\zar}^{\leq}\to{(\mathbf{A}_{k}^{1})}_{\zar}^{\leq}\times{(\mathbf{A}_{k}^{1})}_{\zar}^{\leq}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}

which is a contradiction.
∎

Part II
The condensed fundamental group

The purpose of this part is to analyze the fundamental group of the condensed homotopy type and its relationship to the étale and proétale fundamental groups.
We start by showing that, surprisingly,
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1})
is nontrivial (see
Corollary

7.8
).
This can be viewed as saying that there exists a nontrivial proétale local system of
condensed
rings on
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}
.
See
Example

7.10
.

In
Section

7
, we show that a mild quotient of the condensed fundamental group of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}
indeed becomes trivial.
Specifically, Clausen and Scholze introduced a localization
{A}\mapsto{A^{\qs}}
of the category of condensed sets called the
quasiseparated quotient

[
Sch19
, Lecture VI]
.
For topological groups, this is analogous to the Hausdorff quotient.
We show that if
X
is a topologically noetherian scheme that is geometrically unibranch, then there is a natural isomorphism of condensed groups

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{x})^{\qs}\similarrightarrow\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(X,\bar{x})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

See
Theorem

7.27
.
Under mild hypotheses on the scheme (e.g., being Nagata), we also prove a van Kampen formula for the quasiseparated quotient of the condensed fundamental group that only involves topological free products, topological quotients, and the étale fundamental group of the normalization, see
Theorem

7.51
.

In
Section

8
, we turn to the relationship between the condensed fundamental group and the proétale fundamental group introduced by Bhatt and Scholze
[
BhattScholzeProetale
, §7]
.
One of the special features of
\mathrm{\pi}^{\proet}_{1}(X)
is that it is a
Noohi group
.
We show that if
X
is topologically noetherian, the
Noohi completion
(suitably extended to condensed groups) of
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X)
recovers
\mathrm{\pi}^{\proet}_{1}(X)
, see
Theorem

8.17
.

7
The quasiseparated quotient of the condensed fundamental group

In
Section

7.1
, we begin by using the Galois category description of the condensed homotopy type to show that
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1})
is nontrivial.
The rest of the section is dedicated to studying the quasiseparated quotient of
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1})
.
In
Section

7.2
, we recall the basics on quasiseparated quotients of condensed sets and prove some fundamental results about the quasiseparated quotient.
In
Section

7.3
, we show that the quasiseparated quotient of
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}
of a geometrically unibranch and topologically noetherian scheme recovers
\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}
.
In
Section

7.4
, we prove a van Kampen formula for the quasiseparated quotient of the condensed fundamental group, see
Theorem

7.51
.

7.1
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1})
is nontrivial

In this subsection, we show that
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}
can behave wildly, even in geometrically very simple situations.
For simplicity, we work over the complex numbers
\mathbf{C}
.

7.1 Notation
.

For a topological group
G
and an (abstract) subgroup
H<G
, let
H^{\operatorname{nc}}
denote the group-theoretic
normal closure
of
H
in
G
.
Let

H^{\operatorname{tnc}}\coloneq\overline{H^{\operatorname{nc}}}

be the
topological normal closure
of
H
in
G
, i.e., the smallest
closed
normal subgroup of
G
containing
H
or, equivalently, the topological closure of
H^{\operatorname{nc}}
in
G
.

7.2 Proposition
.

Let
S\subset\mathbf{C}
be a subset.
Let us write

\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}\smallsetminus{S}\coloneq\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C}[t][(t-a)^{-1}\mid a\smallin S])\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Let
\Freepf_{\mathbf{C}}
be the free profinite group on the underlying set of
\mathbf{C}
.
Let
N_{S}
be the abstract normal subgroup of
\Freepf_{\mathbf{C}}
generated by
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}(a)
for all
a\smallin\mathbf{C}\smallsetminus{S}
.
Write
\eta
for the generic point of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}
and
\bar{\eta}
for the geometric generic point induced by choosing an algebraic closure of
\mathbf{C}(T)
.
There is a short exact sequence of (abstract) groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Freepf_{\mathbf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}\smallsetminus{S},\bar{\eta})({\mathbin{*}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}}

To prove
Proposition

7.2
, we make use of an alternative description of
\BGal(X)({\mathbin{*}})
.
To explain this, we first recall that
\mathrm{Gal}(X)({\mathbin{*}})
admits a conservative functor to a poset:

7.3 Example
.

Let
X
be a qcqs scheme.
Note that there is a natural functor

s\colon\mathrm{Gal}(X)({\mathbin{*}})\to{X}_{\zar}^{\leq}

from the category of points of the étale topos to the specialization poset of
|X|
.
The functor
s
is the unique functor that sends a geometric point
\bar{x}\to X
to the underlying point
x\smallin|X|
.
Since the fiber of
s
over a point
x\smallin{X}_{\zar}^{\leq}
is equivalent to the classifying anima of the discrete group
\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathrm{\kappa}(x)}
, the functor
s
is conservative.

Our description thus relies on the following presentation of
\infty
-categories with a conservative functor to a poset:

7.4 Recollection

((
\infty
-categories with a conservative functor to a poset))
.

Let
P
be a poset.
Write
\sd(P)
for the poset of nonempty linearly ordered finite subsets of
P
, ordered by inclusion.
The poset
\sd(P)
is referred to as the
subdivision
of
P
.
Write
\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty,/P}^{\cons}\subset\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty,/P}
for the full subcategory spanned by those
\infty
-categories over
P
such that the structure morphism
\mathcal{C}\to P
is conservative.
Barwick–Glasman–Haine proved that the
nerve
functor

\displaystyle\mathrm{N}_{P}\colon\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty,/P}^{\cons}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\sd(P)^{\op},\textbf{{Ani}})

\displaystyle[\mathcal{C}\to P]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\left[\{p_{0}<\cdots<p_{n}\}\mapsto\mathrm{Map}_{\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty,/P}}(\{p_{0}<\cdots<p_{n}\},\mathcal{C})\right]

is a fully faithful right adjoint.
See
[
BGH20
, Theorem 2.7.4]
.

The next result provides a convenient way of computing the classifying anima
\mathrm{B}\mathcal{C}
in terms of the nerve
\mathrm{N}_{P}(\mathcal{C})
.

7.5 Proposition
.

Let
P
be a poset and
\mathcal{C}\to P
a conservative functor.
Then there is a natural equivalence

\mathrm{B}\mathcal{C}\simeq\colim_{\sd(P)^{\op}}\mathrm{N}_{P}(\mathcal{C})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Proof.

First, observe that the functor
P\times(-)\colon\textbf{{Ani}}\to\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty,/P}
factors through
\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty,/P}^{\cons}
and is right adjoint to the functor
\mathrm{B}\colon\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty,/P}^{\cons}\to\textbf{{Ani}}
sending
\mathcal{C}\to P
to the classifying anima
\mathrm{B}\mathcal{C}
.
Since the colimit functor
\mathrm{Fun}(\sd(P)^{\op},\textbf{{Ani}})\to\textbf{{Ani}}
is left adjoint to the constant functor, in light
7.4
and the diagram of adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\sd(P)^{\op},\textbf{{Ani}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty,/P}^{\cons}}
Ani
,
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{N}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\times(-)}

it suffices to show that the composite right adjoint
\textbf{{Ani}}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\sd(P)^{\op},\textbf{{Ani}})
is equivalent to the constant functor.

To prove this, first note that for nonempty linearly ordered finite subset
\{p_{0}<\cdots<p_{n}\}\subset P
, the classifying anima
\mathrm{B}\{p_{0}<\cdots<p_{n}\}
is contractible.
Hence, for any anima
A
and nonempty linearly ordered finite subset
\{p_{0}<\cdots<p_{n}\}\subset P
, we have natural equivalences

\displaystyle\mathrm{N}_{P}(P\times A)\{p_{0}<\cdots<p_{n}\}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Map}_{\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty,/P}}(\{p_{0}<\cdots<p_{n}\},P\times A)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\textbf{{Ani}}}(\mathrm{B}\{p_{0}<\cdots<p_{n}\},A)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\textbf{{Ani}}}({\mathbin{*}},A)

\displaystyle=A\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}\qed

7.6 Example
.

In particular, if
X
is a qcqs scheme, then there is a natural equivalence

\BGal(X)({\mathbin{*}})\simeq\colim_{\subdiv({X}_{\zar}^{\leq})^{\op}}\mathrm{N}_{{X}_{\zar}^{\leq}}(\mathrm{Gal}(X)({\mathbin{*}}))\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Proof of
Proposition

7.2
.

To simplify notation, write
X=\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}\smallsetminus{S}
,
\mathrm{Gal}(X)
for
\mathrm{Gal}(X)({\mathbin{*}})
, and
\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{Gal}(X))
for
\mathrm{N}_{{X}_{\zar}^{\leq}}(\mathrm{Gal}(X))
.
We compute
\BGal(X)
using
Example

7.6
.
Note that
\subdiv({X}_{\zar}^{\leq})
consists of elements of the form

\{a\}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}\hskip 11.74988pt\{\eta\}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}\text{\hskip 23.49976ptand\hskip 23.49976pt}\{a<\eta\}

for any
a\smallin\mathbf{C}\smallsetminus{S}
, and the ordering is given by
\{a\}<\{a<\eta\}
and
\{\eta\}<\{a<\eta\}
.
Furthermore, the functor

\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{Gal}(X))\colon\subdiv({X}_{\zar}^{\leq})^{\op}\to\textbf{{Ani}}

can be explicitly described by applying
\widehat{\Pi}_{\infty}^{\et}
followed by
\lim\colon\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\mathrm{\pi}})\to\textbf{{Ani}}
to the diagram
\subdiv({X}_{\zar}^{\leq})^{\op}\to\textbf{{Sch}}
that sends
\{a\}<\{a<\eta\}>\{\eta\}

to the span of schemes

(7.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C}[T]_{(a)}^{\mathrm{h}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C}[T]_{(a)}^{\mathrm{h}})\smallsetminus\{a\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C}(T))\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}}}

See
[
BGH20
, Example 12.2.2]
.

For each
a\smallin\mathbf{C}\smallsetminus S
, we now choose a lift
\bar{\eta}_{a}
of
\bar{\eta}
fitting into a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C}[T]_{(a)}^{\mathrm{h}})\smallsetminus\{a\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}(\overline{\mathbf{C}(T)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C}(T))\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\eta}_{a}}

In particular, we can lift the span (
7.7
) to a span of pointed schemes; therefore,
\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{Gal}(X))
also lifts to a diagram of pointed anima
\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{Gal}(X))_{*}
.
Using that
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}
is an equivalence between pointed, connected,
1
-truncated anima and the category of groups
[
HTT
,
Proposition 7.2.12
]
, we may thus compute

\mathrm{\pi}_{1}(\mathrm{B}\mathrm{Gal}(X),\bar{\eta})\simeq\colim_{\subdiv({X}_{\zar}^{\leq})^{\op}}\mathrm{\pi}_{1}(\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{Gal}(X))_{\mathbin{*}})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Now for any
\{a\}<\{a<\eta\}>\{\eta\}
, the corresponding span in groups is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C}[T]_{(a)}^{\mathrm{h}})\smallsetminus\{a\},\bar{\eta}_{a})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C}(T)),\bar{\eta})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}}

Moreover, the colimit of the diagram
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}(\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{Gal}(X))_{\mathbin{*}})
over
\subdiv({X}_{\zar}^{\leq})^{\op}
is given by taking the quotient of
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C}(T)),\bar{\eta})=\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathbf{C}(T)}
by the (abstract) normal closure of the subgroup generated by the images of all the decomposition groups

\mathrm{D}_{a}\coloneq\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C}[T]_{(a)}^{\sh}\smallsetminus{\{a\}})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

By
Theorem

C.3
, there is an isomorphism

\Freepf_{\mathbf{C}}\similarrightarrow\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathbf{C}(T)}=\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C}(T)),\bar{\eta})

from the free profinite group on the set
\mathbf{C}
, under which the preimage of
\mathrm{D}_{a}
is, up to conjugation, given by the profinite subgroup
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}(a)
generated by
a
.
It follows that
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}(\BGal(X),\bar{\eta})
is isomorphic to the quotient of
\Freepf_{\mathbf{C}}
by the smallest (abstract) normal subgroup containing
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}(a)
for all
a\smallin\mathbf{C}\smallsetminus S
, as desired.
∎

7.8 Corollary
.

Let
\bar{x}\to\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}}
be a geometric point.
Then the abelianization of the underlying group
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1},\bar{x})({\mathbin{*}})
is nontrivial.
As a consequence,

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1},\bar{x})\neq 1\text{\hskip 24.00024ptand\hskip 24.00024pt}\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1},\bar{x})^{\mathrm{ab}}\neq 1\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}
is irreducible,
4.21
implies that the condensed fundamental groups of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}
with respect to all basepoints are isomorphic.
So it suffices to treat the case where
\bar{x}=\bar{\eta}
is the geometric generic point.

Consider the canonical continuous homomorphism
\Freepf_{\mathbf{C}}\to\prod_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}
that carries a generator
a
to the unit vector at
a
.
Note that since the image of this is homomomorphism dense, the source is profinite, and the target is Hausdorff, this homomorphism is surjective.
Also notice that that the (abstract) normal subgroup
N_{\varnothing}
lands in the subgroup
\bigoplus_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}
.
Thus, by
Proposition

7.2
, we obtain commutative diagram of abstract groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\varnothing}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Freepf_{\mathbf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1},\bar{\eta})({\mathbin{*}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}}

where the rows are short exact sequences.
Here,
Q\neq 1
denotes the abstract quotient.
Since the middle vertical map is surjective, the right vertical map is also surjective.
Since
Q
is abelian, we deduce that
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1},\bar{\eta})({\mathbin{*}})^{\mathrm{ab}}\neq 1
.
∎

7.9 Example
.

The proof of
Corollary

7.8
also shows that the abelianization of
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}},\bar{x})({\mathbin{*}})
is nontrivial.
Indeed, the argument of the proof of
Proposition

7.2
can be used to show that there is a pushout square of groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}\simeq\mathrm{D}_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}},\bar{x})({\mathbin{*}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}},\bar{x})({\mathbin{*}})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}}

By the proof of
Corollary

7.8
,
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}},\bar{x})({\mathbin{*}})
surjects onto
Q=\big(\prod_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}\big)/\big(\bigoplus_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}\big)
.
It follows that
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}},\bar{x})({\mathbin{*}})
surjects onto
Q/\im(\mathrm{D}_{\infty})
which has the same cardinality as
Q
and is thus nontrivial.

One can also use
Corollary

7.8
to show that for some exotic condensed rings, there are nontrivial lisse sheaves on
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}
.

7.10 Example
.

The forgetful functor
\Cond(\mathbf{Ring})\to\Cond(\textbf{{Ab}})
admits a left adjoint given by applying the group ring functor pointwise and then sheafifying.
Writing
A=\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}},\bar{x})^{\mathrm{ab}}
, we thus obtain a nontrivial condensed ring
\mathbf{Z}[A]
.
Furthermore, there is a canonical action of the condensed group
A
on the free
\mathbf{Z}[A]
-module of rank
1
, given by multiplication.
Using the monodromy equivalence of
Proposition

3.22
, this yields a lisse
\mathbf{Z}[A]
-module on
\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}}
that is
not
constant, i.e., not in the image of the basechange functor

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{lis}}(*_{\proet};\mathbf{Z}[A])\to\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{lis}}(\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{C},\proet};\mathbf{Z}[A])\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

While they fit best in this subsection, the following remark and example use the notion of a
quasiseparated
condensed set.
We recall some background about quasiseparatedness and quasiseparated quotients in
Section

7.2
below; hence the reader might prefer to return to these points after consulting
Section

7.2
.

7.11 Remark
.

The proof of
Corollary

7.8
can be adapted to show more generally that whenever
\mathbf{C}\smallsetminus{S}
is infinite, the condensed group
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}\smallsetminus{S},\bar{\eta})
is not profinite and therefore, by
Theorem

7.27
, also not quasiseparated.
Indeed, if it were, it would follow from
Proposition

7.2
that
N_{S}\subset\Freepf_{\mathbf{C}}
is a closed subgroup.
Thus, the image of
N_{S}
under the map
\Freepf_{\mathbf{C}}\to\prod_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}
would also be closed in
\prod_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}
.
But this image is exactly
\bigoplus_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}\smallsetminus{S}}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}
, which is not closed if
\mathbf{C}\smallsetminus{S}
is infinite.
Even more generally, the above arguments show that for any Dedekind scheme
X
, if the abstract normal closure
N\subset\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathbf{C}(X)}
of the subgroup generated by all decomposition groups is not closed, then the condensed fundamental group of
X
is not quasiseparated.

The next example shows that whenever
S\neq\varnothing
, even if
\mathbf{C}\smallsetminus S
is finite, the condensed fundamental group on
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}\smallsetminus{S}
is not quasiseparated.
For example, this covers the case of the localization
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C}[T]_{(T-a)})
for
a\smallin\mathbf{C}
.
To explain it, we need the following lemma about profinite groups.

7.12 Lemma
.

Let
G=\Freepf_{\{a,b\}}
be the free profinite group on two elements
a
and
b
, and let

H\coloneq\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}(b)\subset G

be the (necessarily free) profinite subgroup of
G
generated by
b
.
Then
H^{\operatorname{nc}}\subsetneq H^{\operatorname{tnc}}
.

Proof.

For each integer
n\geq 1
, let
g_{n}\coloneq\prod_{i=1}^{n}(a^{i!}b^{i!}a^{-i!})
.
For each
n
, we have
g_{n}\smallin H^{\operatorname{nc}}
.
Moreover,
(g_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence in
G
.
To prove that
H^{\operatorname{nc}}\neq H^{\operatorname{tnc}}
, we show that
(g_{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges
to an element outside of
H^{\operatorname{nc}}
.

We first claim that since
G
is Raĭkov-complete, the Cauchy sequence
(g_{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges to some
g\smallin G
.
Indeed, for a given
n_{0}>1
and
n>n_{0}
, we have

g_{n_{0}}^{-1}g_{n}=\prod_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}(a^{i!}b^{i!}a^{-i!})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Let
N\vartriangleleft G
be a normal open subgroup.
Then there exists
n_{0}
such that for any
m\geq n_{0}
, we have
a^{m!},b^{m!}\smallin N
.
This is because
a
and
b
are images of generators of
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}
via (two different) continuous maps
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}\to G
, and the corresponding fact already holds in
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}
.
It now follows that for any
n\geq n_{0}
, the element
g_{n_{0}}^{-1}g_{n}
lies in
N
.
By normality,
g_{n}g_{n_{0}}^{-1}
also lies in
N
.
It follows that
g\smallin H^{\operatorname{tnc}}
.

We want to show that
g\notin H^{\operatorname{nc}}
.
Assume the contrary.
Then there exist some
r\smallin\mathbf{N}
,
c_{i}\smallin G
, and
d_{i}\smallin H
such that
g=\prod_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}d_{i}c_{i}^{-1}
.
Now consider the following system of finite quotients of
G
.
For each
m\geq 1
, let
P_{m}\coloneq(\mathbf{Z}/m!)^{\times m!}
denote the
m!
-fold product of copies of
\mathbf{Z}/m!
, and write

Q_{m}\coloneq P_{m}\rtimes\mathbf{Z}/m!\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}

where the action of
\mathbf{Z}/m!
on
P_{m}
permutes the factors.
Define a homomorphism
G\twoheadrightarrow Q_{m}
by

b\mapsto(\overline{1},0,0,\ldots)\smallin P_{m}=(\mathbf{Z}/m!)^{\times m!}\text{\hskip 23.49976ptand\hskip 23.49976pt}a\mapsto\bar{1}\smallin\mathbf{Z}/m!\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Note that this map sends
g
to
P_{m}
.
Now, for
m\gg r
, we get that, on the one hand, the image of
g
in
P_{m}
has an increasing (with
m
) number of nonzero entries and, on the other hand, the presentation
g=\prod_{i=0}^{r}c_{i}d_{i}c_{i}^{-1}
implies that this number is bounded by
r
.
This is a contradiction.
∎

7.13 Example
.

Let
S\subset\mathbf{C}
be a nonempty subset; we claim that
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{1}\smallsetminus{S},\bar{\eta})
is not quasiseparated.
With the same notation as
Lemma

7.12
, we have a diagram of short exact sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Freepf_{\mathbf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}\smallsetminus{S},\bar{\eta})({\mathbin{*}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\operatorname{nc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Freepf_{\{a,b\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Freepf_{\{a,b\}}/H^{\operatorname{nc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}}

where the middle vertical map sends
z\smallin\mathbf{C}
to
b
if
z\smallin S
and to
a
otherwise.
Then, by construction,
H^{\operatorname{nc}}
is the image of
N_{S}
under this map.
Thus, if
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{1}\smallsetminus{S},\bar{\eta})
were quasiseparated,
N_{S}
would be a closed subgroup (see
Proposition

7.20
below).
Hence so would
H^{\operatorname{nc}}
, contradicting
Lemma

7.12
.

7.14 Remark

((counterexample to “
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}
-properness” of
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}}
))
.

In this remark, we show that

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}})(*)\nsime\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}})(*)

by showing that the cardinality of the former is smaller than that of the latter.
This contrasts with the more classical story of
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}
; see
[
SGA 1
, Exposé X, Théorème 2.6]
and the discussion in
[
Ked17
, §4.1, esp. Lemma 4.1.16]
and
[
SW20
, §16]
.

We have seen in
Example

7.9
that
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}})(*)
admits a quotient with the same cardinality as

\textstyle Q=\big(\prod_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}\big)/\big(\bigoplus_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}\big)\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}

which will provide a lower bound for the cardinality.
On the other hand, as
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}}
is normal, we have seen before that the Galois group of the generic point
\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathrm{\kappa}(\eta)}
surjects onto
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}})(*)
.
By
[
Dou64
, Theorem 2]
,

\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathrm{\kappa}(\eta)}\simeq\Freepf_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

This will provide an upper bound for the cardinality.

We now need to compute the cardinalities of some rather concrete profinite groups.
First, note that
|\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}|=|\mathbf{C}|=2^{\aleph_{0}}
.
It follows that

\big\lvert\textstyle\prod_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}\,\big\rvert=(2^{\aleph_{0}})^{2^{\aleph_{0}}}=2^{\aleph_{0}\cdot 2^{\aleph_{0}}}=2^{2^{\aleph_{0}}}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

We also have

\big\lvert\textstyle\bigoplus_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}\,\big\rvert=\bigg\rvert\displaystyle\colim_{F\subset\mathbf{C}\textup{ finite}}\textstyle\bigoplus_{a\smallin F}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}\,\bigg\rvert\leq|\mathbf{C}|\cdot|\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}|=2^{\aleph_{0}}

Thus,
|Q|=2^{2^{\aleph_{0}}}
.

Now, we want to bound
|\Freepf_{M}|
, where
M
is a countable set (in our case
M=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
).
From the universal property (or see
[
RZ10
, Corollary 3.3.10]
) it follows that

\Freepf_{M}\simeq\lim_{F\subset M\textup{ finite}}\Freepf_{F}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Now (again from the universal property and thanks to the finiteness of the
F
’s), each of the groups
\Freepf_{F}
is just the profinite completion
(\Free_{F})^{\wedge}
of the abstract free group
\Free_{F}
on
F
.
In a finitely generated group, there are only finitely many normal subgroups of a given index.
This implies that the profinite completion of
\Free_{F}
can be written as a countably-indexed inverse limit of finite groups, so
|\Freepf_{F}|=2^{\aleph_{0}}
.
Thus,
|\Freepf_{M}|\leq(2^{\aleph_{0}})^{\aleph_{0}}=2^{\aleph_{0}}
.
Plugging in these bounds, we obtain the desired result.

7.15 Remark

((counterexample to proper base change for proétale sheaves))
.

The results in this subsection can also be used to show that proper base change does not hold for proétale sheaves, even with torsion coefficients prime to the characteristic.
Concretely, we claim that proper base change does not hold for the cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

That is, we claim that the natural transformation

(7.16)

p^{*}f_{*}\to g_{*}q^{*}

of functors
\mathrm{D}_{\proet}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}};\mathbf{F}_{p})\to\mathrm{D}_{\proet}(\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C});\mathbf{F}_{p})
is not an equivalence.
By passing to left adjoints, this is equivalent to the natural transformation
q_{\sharp}g^{*}\to f^{*}p_{\sharp}
being an equivalence.
Note that
p_{\sharp}
is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories.
After plugging in the unit and applying a further
f_{\sharp}
, (
7.16
) being an equivalence would thus imply that there is an equivalence

g_{\sharp}(\mathbf{1})\similarrightarrow f_{\sharp}(\mathbf{1})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Note that we may compute
g_{\sharp}(\mathbf{1})
(and similarly
f_{\sharp}(\mathbf{1})
) explicitly as the
\mathbf{F}_{p}
-homology of the condensed homotopy type
\Pi_{\infty}^{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}})
.
The latter is computed by taking homology pointwise and then sheafifying.
In particular, on global sections
g_{\sharp}(\mathbf{1})({\mathbin{*}})
is simply the
\mathbf{F}_{p}
-homology of the anima
\Pi_{\infty}^{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}})({\mathbin{*}})
.
Since the anima
\Pi_{\infty}^{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}})({\mathbin{*}})
is connected, the universal coefficient theorem implies that

\mathrm{\pi}_{1}(g_{\sharp}(\mathbf{1})({\mathbin{*}}))\simeq\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}},\bar{x})({\mathbin{*}})^{\mathrm{ab}}\otimes\mathbf{F}_{p}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

As in
Remark

7.14
, the latter surjects onto a group with the same cardinality as

\textstyle\big(\prod_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{F}_{p}\big)/\big(\bigoplus_{a\smallin\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{F}_{p}\big)\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}

which is
2^{2^{\aleph_{0}}}
.

On the other hand, we also see that
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}(f_{\sharp}(\mathbf{1})({\mathbin{*}}))
is a quotient of
\Freepf_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}}
an thus its cardinality is at most
2^{\aleph_{0}}
by the computation in
Remark

7.14
.
We conclude that
g_{\sharp}(\mathbf{1})
and
f_{\sharp}(\mathbf{1})
cannot be isomorphic, as desired.

7.2
Preliminaries on quasiseparated quotients

7.17 Recollection
.

A condensed set
A
is
quasiseparated
if for any maps
B\to A
and
B^{\prime}\to A
in which
B
and
B^{\prime}
are quasicompact, the pullback
B\times_{A}B^{\prime}
is quasicompact as well.
We denote by
\Cond(\textbf{{Set}})^{\qs}\subset\Cond(\textbf{{Set}})
the full subcategory that is spanned by the quasiseparated condensed sets.

7.18 Lemma

(
[
Sch19
, Lemma 4.14]
)
.

The inclusion
\Cond(\textbf{{Set}})^{\qs}\subset\Cond(\textbf{{Set}})
admits a left adjoint
(-)^{\qs}
that preserves finite products.

Explicitly, if
A
is a condensed set, its
quasiseparated quotient

A^{\qs}
can be computed by choosing a cover
U=\coprod_{i\smallin I}S_{i}\twoheadrightarrow A
by profinite sets and by defining
A^{\qs}
as the quotient of
U
by the closure of the equivalence relation
U\times_{A}U\subset U\times U
.

Since
(-)^{\qs}
preserves finite products, it induces a functor
\Cond(\textbf{{Grp}})\to\Cond(\textbf{{Grp}})^{\qs}
which is left adjoint to the inclusion.
Our next goal is to derive a more explicit description of the quasiseparated quotient of a condensed group.

7.19 Definition
.

An inclusion
C\subset A
of condensed sets is
closed
if for every profinite set
S
and map
S\to A
, the pullback
C\times_{A}S\subset S
is a closed subspace.

7.20 Proposition
.

Let
G
be a condensed group, and let
\overline{\{1\}}\subset G
denote the intersection of all closed normal subgroups of
G
.
Then there is a natural isomorphism

G^{\qs}\similarrightarrow G/\overline{\{1\}}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

For the proof, we need two auxiliary results.

7.21 Lemma
.

Let
A
be a condensed set and let
R\subset A\times A
be a closed equivalence relation.
Then the quotient
A/R
is quasiseparated.

Proof.

First, let us choose a cover
U=\coprod_{i\smallin I}S_{i}\twoheadrightarrow A
by profinite sets
S_{i}
.
Set

R_{I}\coloneq R\operatornamewithlimits{\times}_{A\times A}(U\times U)

and note that
R_{I}
defines a closed equivalence relation on
U
with the property that the natural map
U/R_{I}\to A/R
is an isomorphism.
Let
\Lambda
be the filtered poset of finite subsets of
I
, and for each
J\smallin\Lambda
, let
U_{J}=\coprod_{j\smallin J}S_{j}
.
Then we can write
U
as the filtered union of the
U_{J}
, and for each
J\subset J^{\prime}
the inclusion
U_{J}\subset U_{J^{\prime}}
is a closed immersion of compact Hausdorff spaces.
Moreover, for each
J\smallin\Lambda
, let us set

R_{J}\coloneq R_{I}\operatornamewithlimits{\times}_{U\times U}(U_{J}\times U_{J})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Then each
R_{J}
defines a closed equivalence relation on
U_{J}
, and, since
\Lambda
is filtered, we have
R=\colim_{J\smallin\Lambda}R_{J}
.
As a consequence, we may identify
\colim_{J\smallin\Lambda}U_{J}/R_{J}\simeq A/R
.
Now since each
R_{J}
is a closed equivalence relation on
U_{J}
, the condensed set
U_{J}/R_{J}
is a compact Hausdorff space.
Moreover, for every inclusion
U_{J}\subset U_{J^{\prime}}
, the induced map
U_{J}/R_{J}\to U_{J^{\prime}}/R_{J^{\prime}}
is injective by construction of
R_{J}
and
R_{J^{\prime}}
and is therefore automatically a closed immersion.
Hence the desired result follows from
[
Sch19
, Proposition 1.2 (4)]
.
∎

7.22 Lemma
.

Let
\varphi\colon G\to H
be a homomorphism of condensed groups.
If
H
is quasiseparated, then
\ker(\varphi)
is a closed subgroup of
G
.

Proof.

Since
\ker(\varphi)
is the inverse image of
\{1\}\subset H
, it suffices to show that
\{1\}
is closed in
H
.
For this, pick any map from a profinite set
S\to H
.
Since
S
and
\{1\}
are quasicompact and
H
is quasiseparated, the fiber product
S\times_{H}\{1\}\subset S
is quasicompact.
Since a subobject of a quasiseparated condensed set is quasiseparated,
S\times_{H}\{1\}
is also quasiseparated.
It follows that
S\times_{H}\{1\}
is compact, and hence a closed subset of
S
, as desired.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

7.20
.

We begin by showing that the quotient
G/{\overline{\{1\}}}
is quasiseparated.
To see this, first note that the map

(7.23)

(\pr_{0},\mathrm{mult})\colon G\times\overline{\{1\}}\to G\times G

is a closed immersion since when composing this map with the isomorphism
G\times G\to G\times G
given by
(g,h)\mapsto(g,g^{-1}h)
, the resulting map can be identified with the product of the identity with the inclusion.
Observe that the map in (
7.23
) is precisely the equivalence relation defining the quotient group
G/{\overline{\{1\}}}
.
Hence the quasiseparatedness of
G/{\overline{\{1\}}}
follows from
Lemma

7.21
.

To complete the proof, we need to show that for every map
\varphi\colon G\to H
of condensed groups in which
H
is quasiseparated, the kernel
\ker(\varphi)
contains
\overline{\{1\}}
. For this, it suffices to check that
\ker(\varphi)
is closed. This is
Lemma

7.22
.
∎

In order to produce short exact sequences on the level of quasiseparated quotients, it is useful to know the following analogue of being a locally cartesian localization for the quasiseparated quotient.

7.24 Proposition
.

Let
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

be a short exact sequence of condensed groups.
If
H
is quasiseparated, the induced sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\qs}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\qs}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

is again exact.

Proof.

Since
H=H^{\qs}
, we only need to show that
N^{\qs}\to G^{\qs}
is injective.
Again since
H
is quasiseparated,
Lemma

7.22
shows that
N\to G
is closed.
Therefore,
\overline{\{1\}}{}^{N}=\overline{\{1\}}{}^{G}
(as subgroups of
G
), and thus

N^{\qs}=N/\overline{\{1\}}{}^{N}\longrightarrow G/\overline{\{1\}}{}^{G}=G^{\qs}

is injective.
∎

We now obtain a fundamental exact sequence of the quasiseparated quotient of the condensed fundamental group.

7.25 Notation
.

Given a scheme
X
and geometric point
{\bar{x}}\to{X}
, we write

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X,\bar{x})\coloneq\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{x})^{\qs}

for the quasiseparated quotient of the condensed fundamental group of
X
.

7.26 Corollary

((fundamental exact sequence on quasiseparated quotients))
.

Let
k
be a field with separable closure
\bar{k}
, let
X
be a qcqs
k
-scheme, and let
\bar{x}\to X_{\bar{k}}
be a geometric point.
If
X
is geometrically connected and
X_{\bar{k}}
has finitely many irreducible components, then the sequence of condensed groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X_{\bar{k}},\bar{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X,\bar{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Gal}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

is exact.

Proof.

Combine
Corollaries

5.7
and
5.8
with
Proposition

7.24
.
∎

7.3
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}
of geometrically unibranch schemes

It is a common theme in arithmetic geometry that various generalizations of
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}
are all equal (and profinite) for normal (more generally: geometrically unibranch) schemes.
See
[
MR0245577
, Theorem 11.1]
and
[
BS15
, Lemma 7.4.10]
for instances of this phenomenon.
As we saw before, this fails for
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}
and
X=\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}}
. However, the expected behavior still holds for
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}
. Proving this fact is the main goal of this subsection.

7.27 Theorem
.

Let
X
be a qcqs geometrically unibranch scheme with finitely many irreducible components, and let
\bar{x}\to X
be a geometric point.
Then the natural homomorphism
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{x})\to\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(X,\bar{x})
induces an isomorphism

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X,\bar{x})\similarrightarrow\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(X,\bar{x})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

In particular,
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X,\bar{x})
is a profinite group.

For the proof, we need the following observation.

7.28 Proposition
.

Let
X
be a qcqs scheme such that
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{0}(X)
is discrete.
Then for any geometric point
\bar{x}\to X
, the natural comparison homomorphism

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{x})\to\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(X,\bar{x})

of
3.15
exhibits
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(X,\bar{x})
as the profinite completion of
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{x})
.
The hypothesis on
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{0}(X)
is satisfied, for example, when
X
has locally finitely many irreducible components.

Proof.

Combine
Lemma

2.12
,
Lemma

3.14
, and
Corollary

4.19
.
∎

To prove the main result, we first want to show that this quasiseparated quotient is a compact topological group.
For this, we make use of the following simple consequence of the fact that the fundamental group of a simplicial set coincides with the fundamental group of its geometric realization:

7.29 Lemma
.

Let
f\colon T_{\bullet}\to S_{\bullet}
be a map of simplicial sets that is bijective on vertices and surjective on edges.
Then, for any choice of basepoint
t\smallin T_{0}
, the induced homomorphism

f_{{\mathbin{*}}}\colon\mathrm{\pi}_{1}(T_{\bullet},t)\to\mathrm{\pi}_{1}(S_{\bullet},f(t))

is surjective.
∎

7.30 Lemma
.

Let
Y\to X
be a morphism of qcqs schemes.
Assume that there exist proétale hypercovers
X_{\bullet}^{\prime}\to X
and
Y_{\bullet}^{\prime}\to Y
by w-strictly local schemes and a morphism
Y^{\prime}_{\bullet}\to X^{\prime}_{\bullet}
that fit into a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\bullet}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\bullet}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

such that:

(1)

The induced map of profinite sets
\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(Y_{0}^{\prime})\to\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X_{0}^{\prime})
is a bijection (and thus, a homeomorphism).

(2)

The induced map of profinite sets
\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(Y_{1}^{\prime})\to\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X_{1}^{\prime})
is a surjection (and thus, a topological quotient map).

Then, for any choice of geometric points
\bar{y}\mapsto\bar{x}
, the induced homomorphism

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(Y,\bar{y})\to\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{x})

is a surjection of condensed groups.

Proof.

By
2.7
,
3.17
, and
3.43
, the fundamental group
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{x})
can be computed as

\textbf{{Extr}}^{\op}\smallni S\mapsto\mathrm{\pi}_{1}\bigg(\colim_{[m]\smallin\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}}\mathrm{Map}_{\textbf{{Top}}}(S,\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X_{m}^{\prime})),\bar{x}\bigg)\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

In other words, for each extremally disconnected profinite set
S
, we have to compute the fundamental group of the simplicial set
\mathrm{Map}_{\textbf{{Top}}}(S,\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X^{\prime}_{\bullet}))
given by
[m]\mapsto\mathrm{Map}_{\textbf{{Top}}}(S,\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X^{\prime}_{m}))
.
Analogous statements hold for
Y^{\prime}_{\bullet}
and
Y
.

The assumptions on the maps
\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(Y^{\prime}_{0})\to\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X^{\prime}_{0})
and
\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(Y^{\prime}_{1})\to\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X^{\prime}_{1})
imply that, for each
S\smallin\textbf{{Extr}}
, the induced map

\mathrm{Map}_{\textbf{{Top}}}(S,\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(Y^{\prime}_{\!\bullet}))\to\mathrm{Map}_{\textbf{{Top}}}(S,\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X^{\prime}_{\bullet}))

of simplicial sets satisfies the assumptions of
Lemma

7.29
.
It follows that, for each
S
, the map

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(Y,\bar{y})(S)\to\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{x})(S)

is a surjection, as desired.
∎

7.31 Lemma
.

Let
X
be a quasiseparated, geometrically unibranch, irreducible scheme and let
\eta\smallin X
be its generic point.
Let
X_{\bullet}
be any proétale hypercover by w-contractible qcqs schemes of
X
.
Then there exists a proétale hypercover
Y_{\!\bullet}
of
\eta
satisfying the conditions of
Lemma

7.30
(with respect to
X_{\bullet}
and the map
\eta\to X
).

Proof.

Let
X_{\bullet,\eta}
be the basechange of
X_{\bullet}
to
\eta
.
Note that by geometrical unibranchness and the fact that each connected component of a w-contractible proétale
X^{\prime}
over
X
is the strict localization at some geometric point of
X
(see, e.g.,
[
LaraHESpaper
, Lemma 3.15]
), the map
\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X_{\bullet,\eta})\to\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X_{\bullet})
is a levelwise homeomorphism.
In particular, the profinite sets
\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X_{i,\eta})
are still extremally disconnected.
Being w-strictly local, however, will usually be lost after base-changing to
\eta
.
We want to define a w-strictly local hypercover
Y_{\!\bullet}
of
\eta
with a map to
X_{\bullet,\eta}
that still has the desired properties on
\mathrm{\pi}_{0}
in low degrees.

To do that, fix a geometric point
\bar{\eta}
lying over
\eta
and write
X_{0,\bar{\eta}}\coloneq X_{0,\eta}\times_{\eta}\bar{\eta}
.
The projection induces a surjective map of profinite sets
\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X_{0,\bar{\eta}})\to\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X_{0,\eta})
.
As the target is extremally disconnected, this map admits a section.
Let
T\subset\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X_{0,\bar{\eta}})
be the image of one such section.
By
[
BhattScholzeProetale
, Lemma 2.2.8]
, there exists a pro-(Zariski localization)
W_{0}\to X_{0,\bar{\eta}}
that realizes the map
T\subset\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X_{0,\bar{\eta}})
on connected components.
Such
W_{0}
is, in particular, weakly étale over
\bar{\eta}
; by
Example

2.47
we deduce that
W_{0}
is w-strictly local.
By construction, the map
\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(W_{0})\to\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X_{0,\eta})
induced by
W_{0}\to X_{0,\bar{\eta}}\to X_{0,\eta}
is a homeomorphism.

We can extend this to a map of hypercovers

Y_{\bullet}\coloneq\cosk_{0}(W_{0})\operatornamewithlimits{\times}_{\cosk_{0}(X_{\bullet,\eta})}X_{\bullet,\eta}\longrightarrow X_{\bullet,\eta}

that induces a bijection on
0
-simplices.
The map on
1
-simplices is explicitly given by

(7.32)

(W_{0}\times_{\eta}W_{0})\operatornamewithlimits{\times}_{X_{0,\eta}\times_{\eta}X_{0,\eta}}X_{1,\eta}\longrightarrow X_{1,\eta}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Since
W_{0}\to X_{0,\eta}
is surjective, we deduce that (
7.32
) is surjective.
Furthermore, all terms of
Y_{\bullet}
are weakly étale over
\bar{\eta}
, hence, by
Example

2.47
, they are w-strictly local.
This completes the proof.
∎

7.33 Corollary
.

Let
X
be a quasiseparated, geometrically unibranch, irreducible scheme with generic point
\eta\smallin X
.
Choose a geometric point
\bar{\eta}
lying over
\eta
.
Then the natural map

\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathrm{\kappa}(\eta)}=\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(\mathrm{Spec}(\mathrm{\kappa}(\eta)),\bar{\eta})\longrightarrow\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{\eta})

is a surjection of condensed groups.

Proof.

Combine
Lemmas

7.30
,
7.31
, and
3.41
.
∎

7.34 Lemma
.

Let
G^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow G
be a surjection of condensed groups.
Assume that
G^{\prime}
is a profinite group.
Then the quasiseparated quotient
G^{\qs}
is a profinite group.

Proof.

Since the quotient of a quasicompact condensed set is quasicompact, the quotient
G^{\qs}
is qcqs.
By
[
CS22
, Proposition 2.8]
, its underlying condensed set is a compact Hausdorff space.
Since the embedding of compact Hausdorff spaces into condensed sets is fully faithful and commutes with products finite products, it follows that
G^{\qs}
is a compact Hausdorff group.
Since
G^{\qs}
also admits a surjection from the profinite group
G^{\prime}
, we deduce that the compact Hausdorff group
G^{\qs}
is itself profinite.
∎

Finally, we are ready to prove the main result of this subsection.

Proof of
Theorem

7.27
.

Note that, since
\ProFinGrp\subset\Cond(\textbf{{Grp}})^{\qs}\subset\Cond(\textbf{{Grp}})
, the profinite completion
G^{\wedge}
of a condensed group
G
factors over the quasiseparated quotient
G^{\qs}
of
G
.
Our assumptions guarantee that every connected component of
X
is irreducible.
By the preceding preparatory results
Corollaries

7.33
and
7.34
, we thus have that
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X,\bar{x})
is already profinite, hence agrees with the profinite completion
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{x})^{\wedge}
.
By
Proposition

7.28
, this latter profinite completion recovers
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(X,\bar{x})
.
This completes the proof.
∎

7.35 Warning
.

It seems like a natural idea to try to extend the notion of quasiseparatedness and quasiseparated quotients to all
condensed anima
, and also extend
Theorem

7.27
from fundamental groups to homotopy types.
However, a sufficiently nicely behaved quasiseparated quotient of condensed anima can
not
exist.
More precisely, there is
no
full subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subset\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})
with the following properties:

(1)

The inclusion
\mathcal{C}\subset\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})
admits a left adjoint
(-)^{\qs}
.

(2)

A condensed set is in
\mathcal{C}
if and only if its is quasiseparated.

(3)

For any quasiseparated condensed group
G
, the condensed anima
\mathrm{B}G
is contained in
\mathcal{C}
.

Indeed, both
\mathrm{B}\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathrm{B}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}
would be contained in
\mathcal{C}
.
Since
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}/\mathbf{Z}
is the fiber of the canonical map
\mathrm{B}\mathbf{Z}\to\mathrm{B}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}
, the condensed set
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}/\mathbf{Z}
would also be contained in
\mathcal{C}
.
But
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}/\mathbf{Z}
is not quasiseparated.

7.4
The van Kampen and Künneth formulas for
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}

The goal of this subsection is to prove a van Kampen formula for the quasiseparated quotient of the condensed fundamental group (
Theorem

7.51
).
We then use this to prove a Künneth formula for this quasiseparated quotient (
Corollary

7.53
).
To do this, we start by analyzing the relationship between free topological groups and free condensed groups as well as free products of topological groups and condensed groups.

7.36 Notation
.

The forgetful functor
\Cond(\textbf{{Grp}})\to\Cond(\textbf{{Set}})
has a left adjoint

\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{(-)}\colon\Cond(\textbf{{Set}})\to\Cond(\textbf{{Grp}})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

For a condensed set
M
, the condensed group
\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{M}
is given more explicitly as the sheafification of the functor

\displaystyle\Free^{\pre}_{M}\colon\Pro(\textbf{{Set}}_{\textup{fin}})^{\op}

\displaystyle\to\textbf{{Grp}}

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\mapsto\Free_{M(S)}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

The free group on
M
comes with a canonical map
M\to\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{M}
in
\Cond(\textbf{{Set}})
.

7.37
.

For a profinite set
T
, we want to compare
\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{T}
with
\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T}
, i.e., the free topological group on
T
(see
[
ArhangelskiiTkachenko
, Chapter 7]
).
Note that, by the universal property of
\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{T}
, there is a canonical homomorphism

\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{T}\to\underline{\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T}}

in
\Cond(\textbf{{Grp}})
.
To do this, we recall some important facts about free topological groups adn free products of topological groups.

7.38 Recollection

((on free topological groups and products))
.

In this recollection,
T
always denotes a topological space and
G_{i}
denote topogical groups.

(1)

Markov showed that for every Tychonoff (=completely regular) space
T
, the free topological group
\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T}
on
T
exists and the unit
\eta\colon T\to\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T}
is a topological embedding.
In addition, the image
\eta(T)
is a free algebraic basis for
G
.
See
[
ArhangelskiiTkachenko
, Theorems 7.1.2 & 7.1.5]
.

(2)

When
T
is compact (more generally,
\mathrm{k}_{\mathrm{\omega}}
), Graev–Mack–Morris–Ordman showed that
\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T}
is the topological colimit of subspaces

(\Free_{T})_{\leq n}=\{\textrm{words of reduced length }\leq n\}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

See
[
ArhangelskiiTkachenko
, Theorem 7.4.1]
.

(3)

By
[
Graev
]
, the underlying set of
*^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}G_{i}
is the abstract free product and if the groups are Hausdorff, their free product is Hausdorff too.

Moreover, when each
G_{i}
is either compact or finitely generated discrete (e.g.,
\mathbf{Z}^{*r}
), by looking at the surjection from a suitable free product (see
Lemma

7.46
below) and using (
1
), it follows that
*^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}G_{i}
is a topological colimit of compact subsets of
bounded words
.
Here, by bounded words we in particular mean that all “letters” from one of the copies of
\mathbf{Z}
sit inside of some interval
[-n,n]
.
See
[
LaraFESpaper
, Remark 4.27]
.

7.39 Recollection
.

In the context of (abstract) free groups on a set
M
(resp., free products of groups
G_{1},\ldots,G_{n}
) we say that
g_{m_{1}}^{r_{1}}\cdots g_{m_{n}}^{r_{n}}
(resp.,
g_{1}\cdots g_{n}
), where
g_{m_{i}}
is the generator corresponding to
m_{i}\smallin M
(resp., where
g_{i}
is a nontrivial element of one of the groups
G_{j(i)}
) is a
reduced word
if for
1\leq i<n
, we have
m_{i}\neq m_{i+1}
(resp.,
j(i)\neq j(i+1)
).

The following result is a nonabelian analogue of
[
Sch19
]
.
The proof essentially follows the one of
loc. cit.

7.40 Proposition
.

Let
T
be a compact Hausdorff topological space.
Then the natural map

(7.41)

\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{T}\to\underline{\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T}}

is an isomorphism.

7.42
.

In the proof, we use the following convention: for a profinite set
S
and
t\smallin T(S)
, we denote by
g_{t}\smallin\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{T}
the element given by the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{T}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

where
T\to\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{T}
is the unit map.

Proof.

First, we want to check that the map (
7.41
) is injective.
Note that this boils down to checking that any section of
\Free^{\pre}_{T}
that maps to
1\smallin\underline{\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T}}
, trivializes after passing to a cover in
\Pro(\textbf{{Set}}_{\textup{fin}})
.

Observe that this is the case for the underlying groups.
Indeed, it is enough to check that the map
\Free_{T(*)}\to\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T}(*)
is injective.
6
6

6

We are using here that evaluating
\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{W}
on
*
as a sheaf is the same as evaluating its defining presheaf.

This follows directly from
7.38
(
1
).

We now treat the injectivity for a general
S\smallin\Pro(\textbf{{Set}}_{\textup{fin}})
.
Assume that
1\neq g\smallin\Free_{T(S)}
maps to
1\smallin\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T}(S)
.
By the previous point, for any
s\smallin S
, the restriction
g(s)\smallin\Free_{T(*)}
is trivial.
Write
g
as a reduced word
g=g_{t_{1}}^{r_{1}}g_{t_{2}}^{r_{2}}\cdots g_{t_{m}}^{r_{m}}
, where now
t_{j}\smallin T(S)
.
All
g_{t_{j}}
are nonzero and, if
m>1
, we have
g_{t_{i}}\neq g_{t_{i+1}}
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
.

If
m=1
, then we plug in any
s\smallin S
to see that
1=g(s)=g_{t_{1}(s)}^{r_{1}}
. But the right hand side cannot be trivial being a generator in the free group raised to a nonzero power – a contradiction.

Assume now that
m>1
.
Let
S_{j}
denote the closed subset of
S
where
t_{j}=t_{j+1}
.
First, note that the
S_{j}
’s (where
1\leq j<m
) jointly cover
S
.
Indeed, if that would not be the case, then any point
s
in the complement would have the property that

1=g(s)=g_{t_{1}(s)}^{r_{1}}g_{t_{2}(s)}^{r_{2}}\cdots g_{t_{m}(s)}^{r_{m}}

is a nontrivial reduced word, a contradiction.

Thus, passing to a finite closed cover of
S
, we can assume that
t_{j}=t_{j+1}
for some
j
, effectively decreasing the “
m
” in the shortest word that
g
can be written as.
By induction, this implies that
g
has to be trivial – a contradiction.

As the proof of injectivity is finished, we now move on to surjectivity.
Consider the map of compact topological spaces

T^{n}\times\{-1,0,1\}^{n}\to(\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T})_{\leq n}

given by
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n},\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n})\mapsto g_{{t_{1}}}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cdots g_{{t_{n}}}^{\varepsilon_{n}}
.
This map is clearly surjective.
It fits into a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{n}\times\{-1,0,1\}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T})_{\leq n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{m}(\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T})_{\leq m}=\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}}

Evaluating at any
S\smallin\textbf{{Extr}}
, and using
[
BhattScholzeProetale
, Lemma 4.3.7]
, this shows the surjectivity of the lower horizontal map (by varying
n
).
∎

7.43 Remark
.

Assume that
S=\lim_{i}S_{i}
is a profinite set with
S_{i}
finite.
Essentially, the same proof strategy (but without having to use the results of
7.38
(
1
)) shows further that
\Free_{S}^{\mathrm{cond}}
and
\underline{\Free_{S}^{\mathrm{top}}}
are isomorphic to the group
\underline{\bigcup_{m}\lim_{i}\left((\Free_{S_{i}})_{\leq m}\right)}
.
This is analogous to the presentation in
[
Sch19
, Proposition 2.1]
.

Now we turn to analyzing free products of condensed and topological group.

7.44 Notation
.

We denote the the coproduct in the category of condensed groups by
*^{\mathrm{cond}}
.
It can be explicitly described as the sheafification of the presheaf
*^{\pre}_{i}G_{i}
given by

\displaystyle\Pro(\textbf{{Set}}_{\textup{fin}})^{\op}

\displaystyle\to\textbf{{Grp}}

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\mapsto*_{i}G_{i}(S)\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

7.45
.

Free products of topological groups
*^{\mathrm{top}}
exist as well.
For
G_{i}\smallin\mathrm{Grp}(\textbf{{Top}})
there is a canonical homomorphism
*^{\mathrm{cond}}_{i}\underline{G_{i}}\to\underline{*^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}G_{i}}
.

In order to compare condensed and topological free products, we first prove an auxiliary lemma.

7.46 Lemma
.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{m}
be compact Hausdorff topological groups and
r\smallin\mathbf{N}
.
Denote by
T=G_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup G_{m}\sqcup\{1,\ldots,r\}
the topological space that is the disjoint union of the the topological groups
G_{1},\ldots,G_{m}
and
r
singletons.
Then the canonical homomorphism

\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{T}\to G_{1}*^{\mathrm{cond}}\cdots*^{\mathrm{cond}}G_{m}*^{\mathrm{cond}}\mathbf{Z}^{*^{\mathrm{cond}}r}.

is surjective.
An analogous fact holds for topological free products.

Proof.

The universal properties of these groups give a homomorphism as above (here, we are mapping each of the
r
points in
T
to
1\smallin\mathbf{Z}
via one of the
r
canonical maps
\mathbf{Z}\to\mathbf{Z}^{*^{\mathrm{cond}}r}
).
This map already exists on the level of the defining presheaves and is surjective there, so the map of sheaves is surjective as well.

We omit the details for the topological counterpart (it uses
7.38
).
∎

7.47 Proposition
.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{m}
be compact Hausdorff topological groups and
r\smallin\mathbf{N}
.
Then the natural map

G_{1}*^{\mathrm{cond}}\cdots*^{\mathrm{cond}}G_{m}*^{\mathrm{cond}}\mathbf{Z}^{*^{\mathrm{cond}}r}\longrightarrow\underline{G_{1}*^{\mathrm{top}}\cdots*^{\mathrm{top}}G_{m}*^{\mathrm{top}}\mathbf{Z}^{*^{\mathrm{top}}r}}

is an isomorphism in
\Cond(\textbf{{Grp}})
.

Proof.

To see the surjectivity, one can either redo the argument in the proof of
Proposition

7.40
or use its statement together with
Lemma

7.46
and the square (with
T=G_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup G_{m}\sqcup*\sqcup\cdots\sqcup*
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Free^{\mathrm{cond}}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*^{\mathrm{cond}}_{i}G_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\Free^{\mathrm{top}}_{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{*^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}G_{i}}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}}

Now, for the injectivity, the argument is very similar to the proof of
Proposition

7.40
.
We can work with
*^{\pre}_{i}G_{i}
. The homomorphism of underlying groups

*_{i}G_{i}(*)\to\big(*^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}G_{i}\big)(*)

is a bijection (see
7.38
).

Now, fix
S\smallin\Pro(\textbf{{Set}}_{\textup{fin}})
and let
g=g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{n}\smallin*_{i}G_{i}(S)
be mapping to
1\smallin\big(*^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}G_{i}\big)(S)
.
Here, each
g_{j}
is in some
G_{\alpha(j)}(S)
and we can assume this presentation of
g
is a reduced word (we assume
m>1
as the case when
m=1
is again easy).
We know that
g(s)\smallin*_{j}G_{j}(*)
is trivial for any
s\smallin S
.

Let
S_{j}
denote the closed subsets of
S
where
g_{j}
vanishes.
First, note that the
S_{j}
’s (where
1\leq j\leq n
) jointly cover
S
.
Indeed, if that’s not the case, then any point
s
in the complement would have the property that
g(s)=g_{1}(s)g_{2}(s)\cdots g_{n}(s)
is a nontrivial reduced word – a contradiction.

But now, passing to the this cover, we have again reduced the length of the presentation of
g
as a word.
We are done by induction.
∎

7.48 Lemma
.

Let
T
be a compactly generated topological space.
Sending a closed subspace
Z\subset T
to
\underline{Z}\to\underline{T}
induces an order-preserving bijection between closed subspaces of
T
and closed condensed subsets of
\underline{T}
.
The inverse is given by sending a closed condensed subset
Z\subset\underline{T}
to
Z({\mathbin{*}})\subset\underline{T}({\mathbin{*}})=T
equipped with the subspace topology.

Proof.

In order to avoid confusion during the proof, we will write
\underline{S}
for the condensed set represented by a profinite set
S
.
We at first check that the inverse defined above is well-defined, that is, that
Z({\mathbin{*}})
is a closed subset of
T
.
We may check this after pulling back along any continuous map
f\colon S\to T
for
S
a profinite set.
Then the pullback
S\times_{T}Z({\mathbin{*}})\subset S
is the subspace given by those
s\smallin S
such that
f(s)\smallin Z({\mathbin{*}})
.
If we alternatively compute the pullback
Z\times_{\underline{T}}\underline{S}
in
\Cond(\textbf{{Set}})
, then
Z\times_{\underline{T}}\underline{S}\subset\underline{S}
is a closed condensed subset by definition.
In particular,
(Z\times_{\underline{T}}\underline{S})({\mathbin{*}})
is a closed subset of
S
.
But
(Z\times_{\underline{T}}\underline{S})({\mathbin{*}})=Z({\mathbin{*}})\times_{T}S
, as subsets of
S
, and thus
Z({\mathbin{*}})
is closed.

Furthermore, for a closed subspace
Z\subset T
, we have
Z=\underline{Z}({\mathbin{*}})
.
So, conversely, let us start with a closed condensed subset
Z\subset\underline{T}
.
Then for any
S\smallin\ProFin
we claim that the subset
Z(S)\subseteq T(S)
is given by those
f\colon\underline{S}\to\underline{T}
such that for all
s\smallin S
,
f(s)\smallin Z({\mathbin{*}})
.
Indeed, since
Z
is a subobject,
f
is in
Z(S)
, if and only if the monomorphism
j\colon Z\times_{\underline{T}}\underline{S}\to\underline{S}
is an isomorphism.
But since
j
is a closed immersion, it follows that
j
is an isomorphism if and only if
j({\mathbin{*}})
is.
But this is the case if and only
f(s)\smallin Z({\mathbin{*}})
for all
s\smallin S
, as claimed.
Since the same description applies to the condensed subset represented by the subspace
Z({\mathbin{*}})
equipped with the closed subspace structure, the claim follows.
∎

7.49 Corollary
.

Let
G
be a topological group and
H\lhd\underline{G}
a normal condensed subgroup.
Assume that
G^{\qs}
is represented by a compactly generated topological group
G_{0}
.
Let

H_{0}\coloneq\im(H\to G\to G^{\qs}\simeq\underline{G_{0}})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Then the canonical homomorphism of condensed groups

\left(G/H\right)^{\qs}\to\underline{G_{0}\big/{\overline{H_{0}({\mathbin{*}})}}}

is an isomorphism.
Here,
\overline{H_{0}({\mathbin{*}})}
denotes the topological closure in
G
.

Proof.

Comparing universal properties, we see that the natural map
\left(G/H\right)^{\qs}\to(G^{\qs}/H_{0})^{\qs}
is an isomorphism.
By
Proposition

7.20
, it follows further that the natural map

(G^{\qs}/H_{0})^{\qs}\to G^{\qs}/\overline{H_{0}}

is an isomorphism.
Now since
G^{\qs}\simeq\underline{G_{0}}
,
Lemma

7.48
shows that
\overline{H_{0}}\simeq\overline{H_{0}({\mathbin{*}})}
, completing the proof.
∎

We now turn to the van Kampen formula.
To do so, we fix some notation.

7.50 Notation
.

Let
X
be a scheme.

(1)

Assume
X
is connected and has finitely many irreducible components.
Write
\nu\colon X^{\nu}\to X
for the normalization and write

X^{2\nu}\coloneq X^{\nu}\times_{X}X^{\nu}\text{\hskip 23.49976ptand\hskip 23.49976pt}X^{3\nu}\coloneq X^{\nu}\times_{X}X^{\nu}\times_{X}X^{\nu}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Assume that
X^{2\nu}
and
X^{3\nu}
also have finitely many irreducible components (this is true, for example, if
X
is Nagata).
Decompose
X^{\nu}=\coprod_{i}X^{\nu}_{i}
into connected components.
Write
\Gamma
for the “dual graph” with vertices
V=\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X^{\nu})
and edges
E=\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X^{2\nu})
, and fix a maximal tree
T
of
\Gamma
.

(2)

We write

\Pi_{1}^{\mathrm{cond}}(X)\coloneq\trun_{\leq 1}\!\Pi_{\infty}^{\mathrm{cond}}(X)\text{\hskip 23.49976ptand\hskip 23.49976pt}\widehat{\Pi}_{1}^{\et}(X)\coloneq\trun_{\leq 1}\!\widehat{\Pi}_{\infty}^{\et}(X)

for the
condensed fundamental groupoid
of
X
and
profinite étale fundamental groupoid
of
X
, respectively.
Here,
\trun_{\leq 1}
denotes
1
-truncation of condensed (resp., profinite) anima.

7.51 Theorem

((van Kampen formula for the quasiseparated fundamental group))
.

In the notation of
7.50
, after making choices of geometric base points and étale paths (as in
[
Stix:vanKampen
, Corollary 5.3]
), there is a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X,\bar{x})\simeq\underline{\big(*^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}(X^{\nu}_{i},\bar{x}_{i})*^{\mathrm{top}}\mathrm{\pi}_{1}(\Gamma,T)\big)/H^{\operatorname{tnc}}}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}

where
H
is the subgroup generated by the following relations:

(1)

For all
e\smallin E
and
g\smallin\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}(e,\bar{x}(e))
we have
\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}(\partial_{1})(g)\vec{e}=\vec{e}\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}(\partial_{0})(g)
.

(2)

For all
f\smallin\mathrm{\pi}_{0}(X^{3\nu})
, we have

\overrightarrow{(\partial_{2}f)}\alpha^{(f)}_{102}(\alpha^{(f)}_{120})^{-1}\overrightarrow{(\partial_{0}f)}\alpha^{(f)}_{210}(\alpha^{(f)}_{201})^{-1}\Big(\overrightarrow{(\partial_{1}f)}\Big)^{-1}\alpha^{(f)}_{021}(\alpha^{(f)}_{012})^{-1}=1\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Here, each
\alpha^{(f)}_{ijk}
is an element of some
\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}(X^{\nu}_{\ell},\bar{x}_{\ell})
and
\overrightarrow{e},\overrightarrow{(\partial_{i}f})\smallin\mathrm{\pi}_{1}(\Gamma,T)
.

Proof.

Combining
Corollary

6.16
, the fact that
1
-truncation is a left adjoint, and
[
arXiv:2207.09256
, Proposition A.1]
, we obtain an equivalence of condensed groupoids

\colim_{[k]\smallin\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}_{\leq 2}}\Pi_{1}^{\mathrm{cond}}(X^{k\nu})\similarrightarrow\Pi_{1}^{\mathrm{cond}}(X)\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

The fixed geometric points and étale paths fix points and paths in
\Pi_{1}^{\mathrm{cond}}(X)(*)
,
\Pi_{1}^{\mathrm{cond}}(X_{i}^{\nu})(*)
, …, so also in any
\Pi_{1}^{\mathrm{cond}}(X)(S)
,
\Pi_{1}^{\mathrm{cond}}(X_{i}^{\nu})(S)
, …for
S\smallin\textbf{{Extr}}
.
By
Corollary

4.19
, these groupoids are connected.
We now want to pass from a statement about fundamental groupoids to a statement involving fundamental groups.
For a fixed
S\smallin\textbf{{Extr}}
, we can apply the usual “discrete” van Kampen formula: see
[
LaraFESpaper
, Theorem 3.7]
for a version for
2
-complexes of Noohi (and so also discrete) groups or
[
Bourbaki:TopologieAlg
, Chapter IV, §5]
, cf. also
[
Stix:vanKampen
]
.
It implies that

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{x})\simeq\big(*^{\mathrm{cond}}_{i}\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X^{\nu}_{i},\bar{x}_{i})*^{\mathrm{cond}}\mathrm{\pi}_{1}(\Gamma,T)\big)/H^{\prime}

where
H^{\prime}
is the normal condensed subgroup that for each
S
is generated by relations analogous relations as in the statement, but where
g\smallin\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(e,\bar{x}(e))(S)
, etc.

Now, passing to quasiseparated quotients and using
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X^{\nu}_{i},\bar{x}_{i})^{\qs}=\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}(X^{\nu}_{i},\bar{x}_{i})
(this is
Theorem

7.27
) together with
Proposition

7.47
and
Corollary

7.49
yields the result.

We have used the following observation to get
g\smallin\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}(e,\bar{x}(e))
as opposed to
g
being an element of
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(e,\bar{x}(e))
or
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(e,\bar{x}(e))
in relation (
1
): although
X^{2\nu}
might not be normal, so
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(e,\bar{x}(e))
might differ from
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(e,\bar{x}(e))
, the maps
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(\partial_{1}),\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(\partial_{0})
have profinite groups as the targets and thus, factorize through the profinite completion of
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(e,\bar{x}(e))
, which is
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(e,\bar{x}(e))
(cf.
Proposition

7.28
).
As the topological normal closure of the image of
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(e,\bar{x}(e))({\mathbin{*}})
inside
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(e,\bar{x}(e)
is the whole group (one uses the universal property of the profinite completion to check this), the set of relations

\{\,\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}(\partial_{1})(g)\vec{e}\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}(\partial_{0})(g)^{-1}\vec{e}^{-1}\,|\,e\smallin E,g\smallin\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}(e,\bar{x}(e))\,\}

is still in
H^{\operatorname{tnc}}
and contains the original set of relations (i.e., a similarly-defined one where
g\smallin\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(e,\bar{x}(e))
), as desired.
∎

7.52 Example
.

Let
k
be a separably closed field.

(1)

Let
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
be normal curves over
k
with fixed closed points
c_{i}\smallin C_{i}
. Let
C=C\sqcup_{c_{1}=c_{2}}C_{2}
be the gluing of these curves along these closed points.
Then

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(C,c)\simeq\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}(C_{1},c_{1})*^{\mathrm{top}}\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}(X_{2},c_{2})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

(2)

Let
C
be the nodal curve over
k
obtained from
\mathbf{P}_{k}^{1}
by identifying
0
and
1
.
Then

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(C,c)\simeq\mathbf{Z}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

For more computations involving the van Kampen formula (but for Noohi groups), see
[
LaraFESpaper
]
.

7.53 Corollary

((Künneth formula for the quasiseparated fundamental groups))
.

Let
k
be a separably closed field and let
X
and
Y
be
k
-schemes such that
X
,
Y
, and
X\times_{k}Y
satisfy the hypotheses of
7.50
.
Let
\bar{z}\to X\times_{k}Y
be a geometric point lying over geometric points
\bar{x}\to X
and
\bar{y}\to Y
.
If
Y
is proper or
\characteristic(k)=0
, then the natural homomorphism of condensed groups

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X\times_{k}Y,\bar{z})\to\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X,\bar{x})\times\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(Y,\bar{y})

is an isomorphism.

To prove this result, one can combine the van Kampen formula for
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}
and the classical Künneth formula for
\mathrm{\pi}^{\et}_{1}
as in the proof of
[
LaraFESpaper
, Proposition 3.29]
, but this would require one to argue using the explicit relations appearing in the van Kampen theorem.
To avoid it, it is beneficial to first apply the classical van Kampen in the groupoid form and only compute the fundamental groups at the very end.
This is how we structure the proof below.

Proof of
Corollary

7.53
.

Fix integral hypercovers
\nu_{X,\bullet},\nu_{Y,\bullet}
by normal schemes of
X
and
Y
.
Their product is again an integral hypercover of
X\times_{k}Y
by
normal schemes.
Apply
\widehat{\Pi}_{\infty}^{\et}(-)
to these diagrams and pass to colimits in
\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})
. The fixed geometric point
\bar{z}
points them.
Then
1
-truncate and apply
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(-)
to both sides. We get a homomorphism of condensed groups

\mathrm{\pi}_{1}\biggl(\colim_{[m]\smallin\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}}\widehat{\Pi}_{1}^{\et}(X_{m}\times Y_{m}),{\mathbin{*}}\biggr)^{\qs}\to\mathrm{\pi}_{1}\biggl(\colim_{[m]\smallin\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}}\widehat{\Pi}_{1}^{\et}(X_{m})\times\widehat{\Pi}_{1}^{\et}(Y_{m}),{\mathbin{*}}\biggr)^{\qs}

Using
[
arXiv:2207.09256
, Proposition A.1]
, we can compute the colimits as colimits over the full subcategory
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}_{\leq 2}\subset\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}
.
Apply the usual Künneth formula for
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}
(c.f.
[
SGA 1
, Exposé X, Corollaire 1.7 & Exposé XII, Proposition 4.6]
or
[
HHW24
, §4]
), which implies that

\widehat{\Pi}_{1}^{\et}(X_{m}\times Y_{m})=\widehat{\Pi}_{1}^{\et}(X_{m})\times\widehat{\Pi}_{1}^{\et}(Y_{m})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}

to get an isomorphism

\mathrm{\pi}_{1}\biggl(\colim_{[m]\smallin\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}_{\leq 2}}\widehat{\Pi}_{1}^{\et}(X_{m}\times Y_{m}),{\mathbin{*}}\biggr)^{\qs}\similarrightarrow\mathrm{\pi}_{1}\biggl(\colim_{[m]\smallin\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}_{\leq 2}}\widehat{\Pi}_{1}^{\et}(X_{m}),{\mathbin{*}}\biggr)^{\qs}\times\mathrm{\pi}_{1}\biggl(\colim_{[m]\smallin\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}_{\leq 2}}\widehat{\Pi}_{1}^{\et}(Y_{m}),{\mathbin{*}}\biggr)^{\qs}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Now, using the equality
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}=\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}
on normal schemes and arguing via the van Kampen formula as in
Theorem

7.51
to replace the fundamental groupoids by groups, we get that, e.g.,

\mathrm{\pi}_{1}\biggl(\colim_{[m]\smallin\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}_{\leq 2}}\widehat{\Pi}_{1}^{\et}(X_{m}),{\mathbin{*}}\biggr)^{\qs}=\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X,\bar{x})

and similarly for
Y
and
X\times Y
. Note that
X^{2\nu},X^{3\nu}
(and similarly for
Y^{\ldots}
) might not be normal, but in the van Kampen formula all maps from
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}
of (connected components) of those schemes will always factor though a profinite group (by normality of
X^{\nu},Y^{\nu}
and
X^{\nu}\times Y^{\nu}
), so we were allowed to replace
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}
by
\widehat{\Pi}_{1}^{\et}
even for those non-normal schemes in the above computation (cf. similar argument appears in the proof of
Theorem

7.51
). This completes the proof.
∎

7.54 Corollary
.

Let
K\supset k
be an extension of separably closed fields, and let
X
be a
k
-scheme satisfying the hypotheses of
7.50
.
If
\characteristic(k)=0
or
X
is proper, then the projection
X_{K}\to X
induces an isomorphism

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X_{K})\similarrightarrow\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X)\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

7.55 Remark
.

In the parlance of
[
Ked17
]
, the property of schemes established in
Corollary

7.54
could be called
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}
-properness
.
As explained in
Remark

7.14
, before passing to quasiseparated quotients, this is already false for
X=\mathbf{P}_{k}^{1}
.

7.56 Remark
.

In the context of anabelian geometry, it is sometimes beneficial to have a version of the Kurosh subgroup theorem available in the category of groups where our fundamental groups live, or at least its corollary: the characterization of maximal finite/compact/… subgroups of a free product as a “vertex subgroup” (i.e., one of the free summands up to conjugation).
See, e.g.,
[
Mochizuki:Semi-graphs
]
.
Proving such a result for the proétale fundamental group seems rather tricky due to the presence of Noohi completions.
For
\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}
, however, this can be done: see
Proposition

7.57
.

7.57 Proposition
.

Let
X
be a scheme and
\bar{x}
a geometric point.
Assume that there are profinite groups
(G_{i})_{i\smallin I}
and an integer
r\smallin\mathbf{N}
such that

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X,\bar{x})\ \simeq\ *^{\mathrm{top}}_{i}G_{i}*^{\mathrm{top}}\mathbf{Z}^{*r}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Let
H
be a compact topological group and
\varphi\colon H\to\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X,\bar{x})
a continuous homomorphism.
Then there exists an index
i
and an element
g\smallin\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond},\qs}_{1}(X,\bar{x})
such that

\im(\varphi)\subset gG_{i}g^{-1}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Proof.

This follows follows from
[
MorrisNickolas:locally-compact-free-products
, Theorem 1]
.
∎

7.58 Remark
.

We expect the assumptions of
Proposition

7.57
to be satisfied, e.g., when
X
