We do not need this observation in this article.

We conclude by proving the main result of this section.

8.17 Theorem
.

Let
X
be a qcqs scheme with finitely many irreducible components
8
8

8

This is equivalent to being qcqs and having
locally
finitely many irreducible components.
and
\bar{x}\to X
a geometric point.
Then there is a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{x})^{{\operatorname{Noohi}}}\simeq\mathrm{\pi}^{\proet}_{1}(X,\bar{x})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Proof.

Since
X
has finitely many irreducible components, by
Corollary

4.19
we may assume that
X
, and therefore
\Pi_{\infty}^{\mathrm{cond}}(X)
, is connected.
It follows from
Theorem

8.6
that we have a chain of natural equivalences

\displaystyle\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathrm{B}\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{x}),\textbf{{Set}}{}^{\ult})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\Pi_{1}^{\mathrm{cond}}(X),\textbf{{Set}}{}^{\ult})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\Pi_{\infty}^{\mathrm{cond}}(X),\textbf{{Set}}{}^{\ult})

\displaystyle\simeq\wLoc(X)_{\leq 0}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{\pi}^{\proet}_{1}(X,\bar{x})\text{-}\textbf{{Set}}

that are compatible with the forgetful functors to
Set
.
Here, the last equivalence follows from the definition of
\mathrm{\pi}^{\proet}_{1}(X,\bar{x})
in
[
BhattScholzeProetale
, Definition 7.4.2]
combined with Lemmas 7.3.9 and 7.4.1 in
loc. cit.
.
Thus
Lemma

8.13
shows that there is a natural equivalence

\mathrm{\pi}^{\mathrm{cond}}_{1}(X,\bar{x})^{\mathrm{top}}\text{-}\textbf{{Set}}\simeq\mathrm{\pi}^{\proet}_{1}(X,\bar{x})\text{-}\textbf{{Set}}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

In particular, both groups have the same Noohi completion.
Since
\mathrm{\pi}^{\proet}_{1}(X,\bar{x})
is Noohi complete
[
BhattScholzeProetale
, Theorem 7.2.5]
, the claim follows.
∎

Appendices

Appendix A
Rings of continuous functions & Čech–Stone compactification

by Bogdan Zavyalov

The main goal of this section is to provide the crucial input for the computation of the condensed shape of rings of continuous functions in
Section

4.3
.
Namely, we give a self-contained account for the identification (see
Theorem

A.30
below) of the Čech–Stone compactification of a topological space
X
with the maximal spectrum of the ring of continuous functions on
X
.

This identification has already been established in
[
DO71
]
using the notion of pm-ring. In this appendix, we follow the ideas already present in
[
DO71
]
. We do not claim originality of any results in this appendix. Instead, we hope that this appendix gives a self-contained and reader-friendly exposition of some ideas from
[
DO71
]
and
[
GJ76
]
. See also
[
Vec92
,
Vec94
,
Vec96
]
.

Throughout this appendix, we denote by
\mathbf{R}
(resp.,
\mathbf{C}
) the topological ring of real numbers (resp. complex numbers) with the Euclidean topology.
For a topological space
X
, we denote by
\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
(resp.,
\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})
) the ring of real-valued (resp., complex-valued) continuous functions on
X
.

A.1
Main constructions

The main goal of this subsection is to introduce some constructions that will be used in the rest of this appendix. We also study their basic properties.

A.1 Construction
.

Let
X
be a topological space.

(1)

For each point
x\smallin X
, we define the
evaluation functional

\ev_{x}\colon\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\to\mathbf{R}
by the formula

\ev_{x}(f)\coloneq f(x)\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

(2)

We define the map

\iota_{X}\colon X\to\mathrm{Spec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)

to be the unique map that sends each point
x\smallin X
to
\ker(\ev_{x})
.

A.2 Remark
.

The map
\iota_{X}
is clearly natural in
X
.

For our later convenience, we record some basic properties of
\iota_{X}
.

A.3 Lemma
.

Let
X
be a topological space.

(1)

The natural map
\iota_{X}\colon X\to\mathrm{Spec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)
is continuous;

(2)

the image of
\iota_{X}(X)\subset\mathrm{Spec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)
is a dense subset;

(3)

the map
\iota_{X}
factors through
\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)
.

Proof.

In order to see the first claim, it suffices to show that
\iota_{X}^{-1}\big(\mathrm{D}(f)\big)
is an open subset of
X
for every
f\smallin\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
. This follows immediately from the formula
\iota_{X}^{-1}\big(\mathrm{D}(f)\big)=\{x\smallin X\ |\ f(x)\neq 0\}
and the assumption that
f
is continuous.

Now we prove the second claim. Let
Z\coloneq\mathrm{V}(I)\subset\mathrm{Spec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)
be a closed subset containing
\iota_{X}(X)
.
Then the construction of
\iota_{X}
implies that, for every
f\smallin I
, we have
0=\ev_{x}(f)=f(x)
for all
x\smallin X
. Thus
I=0
, and so we conclude that
Z=\mathrm{V}(0)=\mathrm{Spec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)
.

To justify the last claim, it is enough to prove that
\ker(\ev_{x})
is a maximal ideal for every
x\smallin X
. For this, it suffices to show that
\ev_{x}
is surjective. Fix a constant
c\smallin\mathbf{R}
and denote by
\underline{c}
the corresponding constant function on
X
. Then the surjectivity of
\ev_{x}
follows immediately from the observation that
\ev_{x}(\underline{c})=c
.
∎

A.4 Remark
.

In what follows, we also denote by
\iota_{X}
the restriction
\iota_{X}\colon X\to\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)
.

Later in this appendix we show that if
X
is a compact Hausdorff space, then
\iota_{X}
is a homeomorphism.
See
Theorem

A.30
.

A.5 Warning
.

The map
\iota_{X}
is neither injective nor surjective for a general topological space
X
.

A.2
pm-rings

In this subsection, we introduce the notion of
pm-rings
following
[
DO71
]
. Then we show that the natural inclusion
\mathrm{MSpec}(A)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Spec}(A)
admits a continuous retraction for a pm-ring
A
. As a consequence, we deduce that
\mathrm{MSpec}(A)
is a compact Hausdorff space for any pm-ring
A
.
We use the results of this subsection to relate the Čech–Stone compactification of an arbitrary topological space
X
to the maximal spectrum of the ring of continuous functions on
X
.

A.6 Definition

(
[
DO71
]
)
.

A ring
A
is a
pm-ring
if every prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}\subset A
is contained in a unique maximal ideal
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{p}}\subset A
.

A.7 Definition
.

For a pm-ring
A
, we define the
retract map

r_{A}\colon\mathrm{Spec}(A)\to\mathrm{MSpec}(A)
as the unique map that sends a point
x
to its unique closed specialization (equivalently, it sends each prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
to the unique maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{p}}
containing
\mathfrak{p}
). When there is no possibility of confusion, we will denote the map
r_{A}
simply by
r
.

A.8 Remark
.

Below, we present a proof that
r_{A}
is always continuous for a pm-ring
A
. This beautiful proof is due to de Marco and Orsatti.
However, we want to emphasize that, a priori, it is absolutely not clear whether the map
r_{A}
has to be continuous or not. In fact, the author finds it quite surprising and is not aware of any one-line proof of this fact.

A.9 Theorem

(
[
DO71
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Let
A
be a pm-ring.
Then
r\colon\mathrm{Spec}(A)\to\mathrm{MSpec}(A)
is a continuous retraction of the natural embedding
\iota\colon\mathrm{MSpec}(A)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Spec}(A)
.

In fact,
[
DO71
, Theorem 1.2]
shows that
A
is a pm-ring if and only if
\iota
admits a continuous retract (and
r
is the unique continuous retract in this case). However, since we never need the other direction and it is significantly easier, we decided not to include it in this exposition.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we denote by
\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Spec}}(I)\subset\mathrm{Spec}(A)
the vanishing locus of an ideal
I
inside
\mathrm{Spec}(A)
, and by
\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Max}}(I)\coloneq\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Spec}}(I)\cap\mathrm{MSpec}(A)
the vanishing locus of
I
inside
\mathrm{MSpec}(A)
.

By construction, we know that
r\circ\iota=\operatorname{id}
.
So the only thing we really need to show is that the map
r
is continuous.
We fix a closed subset
Z\subset\mathrm{MSpec}(A)
and define

I\coloneq\bigcap_{\mathfrak{m}\smallin Z}\mathfrak{m}\text{\hskip 23.49976ptand\hskip 23.49976pt}J\coloneq\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{p}\smallin r^{-1}(Z)\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{p}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

For the purpose of proving continuity of
r
, it is enough to show that
r^{-1}(Z)=\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Spec}}(J)
. Clearly,
r^{-1}(Z)\subset\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Spec}}(J)
. Therefore, after unravelling all the definitions, we see that it suffices to show that, for any prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}\subset A
such that
J\subset\mathfrak{p}
, we have
r(\mathfrak{p})\smallin Z
.

Step 1: We show
Z=\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Max}}(I)
.

Since
Z
is closed, we know that
Z=\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Max}}(K)
for some ideal
K\subset A
.
By construction, for any
\mathfrak{m}\smallin Z
, we have
K\subset\mathfrak{m}
.
In particular,
K\subset I=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{m}\smallin Z}\mathfrak{m}
.
Thus,
\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Max}}(I)\subset\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Max}}(K)=Z
. On the other hand, the definition of
I
implies that
Z\subset\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Max}}(I)
. Therefore, we conclude that

\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Max}}(I)\subset\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Max}}(K)=Z\subset\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Max}}(I)\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

This implies that
\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{Max}}(I)=Z
.

Now we set
M\coloneq\bigcup_{\mathfrak{m}\smallin Z}\mathfrak{m}
. We note that
1\notin M
, so
M\neq A
. We warn the reader that the set
M
is not generally an ideal in
A
.

Step 2: Let
\mathfrak{p}\subset M
be a prime ideal in
A
. Then
r(\mathfrak{p})\smallin Z
.

Since
\mathfrak{p}\subset M
and
I=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{m}\smallin Z}\mathfrak{m}
, we conclude that
\mathfrak{p}+I\subset M\neq A
.
Thus, we can find a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{n}\subset A
such that

\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{p}+I\subset\mathfrak{n}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Therefore,
r(\mathfrak{p})=\mathfrak{n}
.
Since
I\subset\mathfrak{n}
, Step 1 ensures that
\mathfrak{n}\smallin Z
.
This shows that
r(\mathfrak{p})\smallin Z
.

Step 3: Let
J\subset\mathfrak{p}
be a prime ideal in
A
. Then
r(\mathfrak{p})\smallin Z
.
Since each prime ideal is contained in a unique maximal ideal, it suffices to find a prime ideal
\mathfrak{q}\subset\mathfrak{p}
such that
\mathfrak{q}\subset M
; then Step 2 implies that
r(\mathfrak{p})=r(\mathfrak{q})\smallin Z
.

Now we choose any
t\smallin A\smallsetminus\mathfrak{p}
and
s\smallin A\smallsetminus M
.
Then
ts\neq 0
since otherwise it would imply that

t\smallin\bigcap_{\mathfrak{m}\smallin Z}\mathfrak{m}=J\subset\mathfrak{p}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Hence, the multiplicative system

S=\{\,ts\,|\,t\smallin A\smallsetminus\mathfrak{p}\text{ and }s\smallin A\smallsetminus M\,\}

does not contain
0
.
Therefore, the localization
A[S^{-1}]
is nonzero.
Thus, any maximal ideal in
A[S^{-1}]
defines a prime ideal
\mathfrak{q}\subset A
disjoint from
S
.
Since
1\smallin A\smallsetminus\mathfrak{p}
and
1\smallin A\smallsetminus M
, we conclude that
\mathfrak{q}\subset\mathfrak{p}\cap M
, finishing the proof.
∎

A.10 Corollary
.

Let
A
be a pm-ring.
Then
\mathrm{MSpec}(A)
is a compact Hausdorff space.

Proof.

Theorem

A.9
implies that
r\colon\mathrm{Spec}(A)\to\mathrm{MSpec}(A)
is a continuous surjection.
Since
\mathrm{Spec}(A)
is quasicompact and images of quasicompact spaces are quasicompact,
\mathrm{MSpec}(A)
is seen to be quasicompact.

Now we show that
\mathrm{MSpec}(A)
is Hausdorff. First,
[
STK
,
Tag 0904
]
implies that it suffices to show that, for any two closed points
x,y\smallin\mathrm{Spec}(A)
, there does not exist a point
z\smallin\mathrm{Spec}(A)
which specializes to both
x
and
y
. This follows immediately from the fact that every point of
\mathrm{Spec}(A)
specializes to a unique closed point.
∎

A.11 Definition
.

Let
f\colon A\to B
be a homomorphism between pm-rings. We define the
induced map of maximal spectra

\mathrm{MSpec}(f)\colon\mathrm{MSpec}(B)\to\mathrm{MSpec}(A)
as the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MSpec}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MSpec}(A)\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{A}}

A.12 Warning
.

In general, for a ring homomorphism
A\to B
, the induced map of spectra
\mathrm{Spec}(f)\colon\mathrm{Spec}(B)\to\mathrm{Spec}(A)
does not send
\mathrm{MSpec}(B)
to
\mathrm{MSpec}(A)
.
This does not even hold for a general homomorphism of pm-rings.
Indeed, consider a rank
2
valuation ring
V
with fraction field
K
and a rank-
1
localization
\mathcal{O}
. Then the map
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O})\to\mathrm{Spec}(V)
, induced by the inclusion
V\subset\mathcal{O}
, sends the closed point of
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O})
to a non-closed point of
\mathrm{Spec}(V)
.

A.3
Rings of continuous functions

The main goal of this section is to show that the rings of continuous functions
\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
and
\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})
are pm-rings for any topological space
X
. This will be the crucial ingredient in showing that the Čech–Stone compactification
\mathrm{\beta}(X)
is homeomorphic to
\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)
.

We do not claim originality of any results of this subsection. In fact, our presentation that
\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
is a pm-ring follows
[
GJ76
, Theorem 2.11]
quite closely. The case of
\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})
seems to be missing in
[
GJ76
]
.

Throughout the section, we fix a topological space
X
.

A.13 Definition
.

Let
f\smallin\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
be a continuous function.
Its
vanishing locus
is the set

\mathrm{V}_{X}(f)\coloneq\{\,x\smallin X\,|\,f(x)=0\,\}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

A.14 Definition
.

For a subset
S\subset\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
, the
collection of its zero sets
is the subset

\mathrm{V}_{X}[S]\coloneq\{\mathrm{V}_{X}(f)\ |\ f\smallin S\}\subset\Sub(X)

of the set of all vanishing loci of elements in
S
.
9
9

9

We denote by
\Sub(X)
the set of all subsets of
X
.

For brevity, we put
\mathrm{V}_{X}[X]\coloneq\mathrm{V}_{X}[\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})]
for the set of all vanishing loci of continuous functions on
X
.

A.15 Lemma

(
[
GJ76
, Theorem 2.3]
)
.

Let
I\subset\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
be an ideal and let
Z_{1},Z_{2}\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[I]
. Then

(1)

Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[I]
;

(2)

if
Z\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[X]
and
Z_{1}\subset Z
, then
Z\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[I]
.

Proof.

Let
Z_{1}=\mathrm{V}_{X}(f_{1})
,
Z_{2}=\mathrm{V}_{X}(f_{2})
, and
Z=\mathrm{V}_{X}(f)
for
f_{1},f_{2}\smallin I
and
f\smallin\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
.
For the first claim, note that

Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}=\mathrm{V}_{X}(f_{1})\cap\mathrm{V}_{X}(f_{2})=\mathrm{V}_{X}(f_{1}^{2}+f_{2}^{2})\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[I].

The second claim follows immediately from the observation that

Z=Z_{1}\cup Z=\mathrm{V}_{X}(f_{1})\cup\mathrm{V}_{X}(f)=\mathrm{V}_{X}(f_{1}f)\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[I].\qed

A.16 Definition
.

An ideal
I\subset\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
is a
zs
-ideal
if
\mathrm{V}_{X}(f)\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[I]
implies
f\smallin I
.

A.17 Remark
.

Often,
zs
-ideals are called
z
-ideals.

A.18 Theorem

(
[
GJ76
, Theorem 2.5]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
be a maximal ideal.
Then
\mathfrak{m}
is a
zs
-ideal.

Proof.

We denote by
I_{\mathfrak{m}}\subset\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
the subset of continuous functions whose vanishing locus is equal to a vanishing locus of a function in
\mathfrak{m}
, i.e.,

(A.19)

I_{\mathfrak{m}}\coloneq\{f\smallin\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\ |\ \mathrm{V}_{X}(f)\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[\mathfrak{m}]\}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Now
Lemma

A.15
implies that
I_{\mathfrak{m}}
is an ideal. We pick continuous functions
f,g\smallin I_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
h\smallin\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
and wish to show that
f+g\smallin I_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
fh\smallin I_{\mathfrak{m}}
.
The former claim follows from the observation
\mathrm{V}_{X}(f+g)\supset\mathrm{V}_{X}(f)\cap\mathrm{V}_{X}(g)
and
Lemma

A.15
, while the latter claim follows from the observation
\mathrm{V}_{X}(fh)\supset\mathrm{V}_{X}(f)
and
Lemma

A.15
.

Now
Equation

A.19
implies that, for the purpose of showing that
\mathfrak{m}
is a
zs
-ideal, it suffices to show that
\mathfrak{m}=I_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Clearly, we have
\mathfrak{m}\subset I_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Therefore, the fact that
\mathfrak{m}
is a maximal ideal implies that, in order to show that
\mathfrak{m}=I_{\mathfrak{m}}
, it suffices to show that
1\notin I_{\mathfrak{m}}
. This is equivalent to showing that
\varnothing\notin\mathrm{V}_{X}[\mathfrak{m}]
. For this note that any
f\smallin\mathfrak{m}
is not invertible, therefore
\varnothing\neq\mathrm{V}_{X}(f)
. This finishes the proof.
∎

A.20 Lemma
.

Let
I,J\subset\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
be two
zs
-ideals. Then
I
is a radical ideal and
I\cap J
is a
zs
-ideal.

Proof.

We start with the first claim. Suppose
f\smallin\mathrm{rad}(I)
, so
f^{n}\smallin I
for some
n
. Then we note that
\mathrm{V}_{X}(f)=\mathrm{V}_{X}(f^{n})
. So the definition of a
zs
-ideal implies that
f\smallin I
. In other words,
I
is radical.

Now we deal with the second claim. We first claim that
\mathrm{V}_{X}[I\cap J]=\mathrm{V}_{X}[I]\cap\mathrm{V}_{X}[J]
. We always have an inclusion
\mathrm{V}_{X}[I\cap J]\subset\mathrm{V}_{X}[I]\cap\mathrm{V}_{X}[J]
, so it suffices to show that
\mathrm{V}_{X}[I]\cap\mathrm{V}_{X}[J]\subset\mathrm{V}_{X}[I\cap J]
. Pick
Z\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[I]\cap\mathrm{V}_{X}[J]
. By definition, this means that there are elements
f\smallin I
and
g\smallin J
such that
Z=\mathrm{V}_{X}(f)=\mathrm{V}_{X}(g)
. Since
J
is a
zs
-ideal, it implies that
f\smallin J
. Therefore,
f\smallin I\cap J
and, hence,
Z\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[I\cap J]
.

Now let
f\smallin\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
be a continuous function such that
\mathrm{V}_{X}(f)\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[I\cap J]=\mathrm{V}_{X}[I]\cap\mathrm{V}_{X}[J]
. Then we use the fact that both
I
and
J
are
zs
-ideals to conclude that
f\smallin I\cap J
, i.e.,
I\cap J
is a
zs
-ideal.
∎

A.21 Remark
.

Lemma

A.20
implies that the ideal
(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{R}})\smallin\mathrm{C}(\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R})
is
not
a
zs
-ideal.

A.22 Lemma

(
[
GJ76
, Theorem 2.9]
)
.

Let
I\subset\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
be a
zs
-ideal.
Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

The ideal
I
is prime.

(2)

The ideal
I
contains a prime ideal.

(3)

For any
f,g\smallin\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
such that
fg=0
, we have
f\smallin I
or
g\smallin I
.

(4)

For every
f\smallin\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
, there is a subset
Z\subset X
such that
Z\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[I]
and
f|_{Z}
does not change its sign.

Proof.

The implications (
1
)
\Rightarrow
(
2
) and (
2
)
\Rightarrow
(
3
) are trivial.

Now we show (
3
)
\Rightarrow
(
4
). We start by considering the continuous functions
f^{+}\coloneq\max(f,0)
and
f^{-}\coloneq\min(f,0)
.
Then clearly we have

f^{+}\cdot f^{-}=0\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}

so we have
f^{+}\smallin I
or
f^{-}\smallin I
.
Suppose
f^{+}\smallin I
(the other case is similar), then we can choose

Z\coloneq\{x\smallin X\ |\ f(x)\leq 0\}=\mathrm{V}_{X}(f^{+})\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[I]\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Now we show (
4
)
\Rightarrow
(
1
). We pick two continuous functions
f,g\smallin\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
such that
fg\smallin I
and wish to show that
f\smallin I
or
g\smallin I
. For this, we consider the continuous function
h=|f|-|g|
.
Our assumption implies that there is a zero set
Z\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[I]
such that
h|_{Z}
is, say, nonnegative (the other case is similar). Note that if
f(x)=0
and
x\smallin Z
, then
h(x)=-|g(x)|\geq 0
. Hence,
h(x)=g(x)=0
for such
x\smallin X
. So we conclude that
Z\cap\mathrm{V}_{X}(fg)=Z\cap(\mathrm{V}_{X}(f)\cup\mathrm{V}_{X}(g))=Z\cap\mathrm{V}_{X}(g)
. Therefore, we see that
\mathrm{V}_{X}(g)\smallin\mathrm{V}_{X}[I]
by virtue of
Lemma

A.15
and the following sequence of inclusions:

\mathrm{V}_{X}(g)\supset Z\cap\mathrm{V}_{X}(g)=Z\cap\mathrm{V}_{X}(fg)

Therefore, we conclude that
g\smallin I
since
I
is a
zs
-ideal.
∎

We are almost ready to show that
\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
is a pm-ring.
For the proof, we need the following result from commutative algebra.

A.23 Lemma
.

Let
R
be a ring and let
\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2}\subset R
be prime ideals such that neither of them is contained in the other.
Then
\mathfrak{p}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{p}_{2}
is not a prime ideal.

Proof.

Choose
t\smallin\mathfrak{p}_{1}\smallsetminus\mathfrak{p}_{2}
and
s\smallin\mathfrak{p}_{2}\smallsetminus\mathfrak{p}_{1}
.
Then
st\smallin\mathfrak{p}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{p}_{2}
but
s\notin\mathfrak{p}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{p}_{2}
and
t\notin\mathfrak{p}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{p}_{2}
.
∎

A.24 Theorem

(
[
GJ76
, Theorem 2.11]
)
.

For any topological space
X
, the ring
\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
is a pm-ring.
Hence
\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)
is a compact Hausdorff topological space.

Proof.

Note that the second claim follows from the first and
Corollary

A.10
.
For the first, since every prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
is contained in some maximal ideal, so it suffices to show that
\mathfrak{p}
cannot be contained in two different maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
.
We set
I\coloneq\mathfrak{m}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{m}_{2}
. Then
Theorem

A.18
and
Lemma

A.20
imply that
I
is a
zs
-ideal. By construction, we have an inclusion
\mathfrak{p}\subset I
. Therefore,
Lemma

A.22
ensures that
I
is a prime ideal. However, this contradicts
Lemma

A.23
. Hence, there is only one maximal ideal containing
\mathfrak{p}
.
∎

We now prove that that
\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})
is a pm-ring.
We need some preparatory lemmas.

A.25 Lemma
.

The canonical map
\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}\to\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

First, we note that the assertion is equivalent to showing that the canonical map
\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\oplus i\cdot\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\to\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})
is an isomorphism. In other words, we need to show that any continuous function
f\smallin\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})
can be uniquely written as
f=g+i\cdot h
with
g,h\smallin\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
. Uniqueness is clear. To see existence, we note that
f=\mathrm{Re}(f)+i\cdot\mathrm{Im}(f)
.
∎

A.26 Lemma
.

The canonical map
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C}))\to\mathrm{Spec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R}))
restricts to a bijection

c\colon\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C}))\to\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R}))\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Proof.

By
Lemma

A.25
,
\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\to\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})
is a finite ring extension and thus
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C}))\to\mathrm{Spec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R}))
maps closed points to closed points.
To show that it restricts to a bijection on closed points, it suffices to see that for every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
with residue field
\mathrm{\kappa}(\mathfrak{m})
, the tensor product
\mathrm{\kappa}(\mathfrak{m})\otimes_{\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})}\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})
is a field.
By
Lemma

A.25
, this is equivalent to showing that
\mathrm{\kappa}(\mathfrak{m})\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}
is a field.
For this it suffices to show that the equation
X^{2}+1=0
has no solutions in
\mathrm{\kappa}(\mathfrak{m})
. In other words, we need to show that there are no continuous functions
f\smallin\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
and
g\smallin\mathfrak{m}
such that
f^{2}=-1+g
.
Suppose that such functions exist. Then we note that
g
is not an invertible function since it lies in a maximal ideal. Therefore, there is a point
x\smallin X
such that
g(x)=0
. Thus, we see that
f(x)^{2}=-1+g(x)=-1
. Contradiction, so no such functions exist.
∎

A.27 Corollary
.

For any topological space
X
, the ring
\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})
is a pm-ring.
Hence
\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})\big)
is a compact Hausdorff topological space.

Proof.

Note that the second claim follows form the first and
Corollary

A.10
.
For the first, let
\mathfrak{P}\subset\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})
be a prime ideal and let
\mathfrak{M}\subset\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})
be a maximal ideal containing
\mathfrak{P}
. We put
\mathfrak{p}\coloneq\mathfrak{P}\cap\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
and we set
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
to be the unique maximal ideal containing
\mathfrak{p}
.
Since
\mathrm{Spec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})\big)\to\mathrm{Spec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)
is a finite morphism (see
Lemma

A.25
), it sends closed points to closed points. So we conclude that
\mathfrak{M}\cap\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})=\mathfrak{m}
.
Thus the claim follows from
Lemma

A.26
.
∎

A.28 Corollary
.

The canonical map
c\colon\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C}))\to\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R}))
is a homeomorphism.

Proof.

By
Theorems

A.24
and
A.27
the source and target are both compact Hausdorff spaces, so the claim follows from
Lemma

A.26
.
∎

A.4
Čech–Stone compactification via algebraic geometry

In this subsection, we show that, for any topological space
X
, the compact Hausdorff space
\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R}))
satisfies the universal property of the Čech–Stone compactification of
X
.

A.29 Definition
.

The
Čech–Stone compactification
of a topological space
X
is a pair
(\mathrm{\beta}(X),i_{X})
of a compact Hausdorff space
\mathrm{\beta}(X)
and a continuous map
i_{X}\colon X\to\mathrm{\beta}(X)
such that, for every other compact Hausdorff space
Y
with a continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
, there is a unique continuous map
\mathrm{\beta}(f)\colon\mathrm{\beta}(X)\to Y
satisfying
f=\mathrm{\beta}(f)\circ i_{X}
.

We recall (see
A.1
) that, for every topological space
X
, we have the natural map
\iota_{X}\colon X\to\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R}))
.
We also write
\iota_{X\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}}\colon X\to\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C}))
for the composition of
\iota_{X}
followed by the inverse of
c\colon\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R}))\to\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C}))
.
Our goal is to show that both
\Big(\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big),\iota_{X}\Big)
and
\Big(\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})\big),\iota_{X\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}}\Big)
are Čech–Stone compactifications of
X
.

A.30 Theorem
.

Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space.
Then all maps in the commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

are homeomorphisms.

Proof.

The diagram commutes by construction and
c
is a homeomorphism by
Corollary

A.28
.
It thus suffices to show that
\iota_{X}
is a homeomorphism.

Step 1:
\iota_{X}
is injective.

To show injectivity of
\iota_{X}
, it suffices to show that any two different points
x,y\smallin X
can be separated by a continuous function
f\colon X\to\mathbf{R}
.
More precisely, we need to find a continuous function
f\colon X\to\mathbf{R}
such that
f(x)=0
and
f(y)\neq 0
.
Such a function exists by Urysohn’s Lemma
[
Mun00
, Theorem 33.1]
.

Step 2:
\iota_{X}
has dense image.
This follows directly from
Lemma

A.3
.

Step 3:
\iota_{X}
is a homeomorphism.

Since
X
is quasi-compact, we conclude that its image
\iota_{X}(X)
is also quasi-compact.
Since
\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R}))
is Hausdorff (see
Theorem

A.24
), we conclude that
\iota_{X}(X)
is closed.
Since
\iota_{X}(X)\subset\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R}))
is dense, we conclude that
\iota_{X}
must be surjective.
Therefore,
\iota_{X}
is a bijective continuous map between compact Hausdorff spaces (see
Theorem

A.24
), so it is a homeomorphism in virtue of
[
STK
,
Tag 08YE
]
.
∎

A.31 Lemma
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a continuous map of topological spaces.
Then there is a unique continuous map
\widetilde{f}\colon\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)\to\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(Y,\mathbf{R})\big)
that makes the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(Y,\mathbf{R})\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}

commute.

Proof.

First, we note that
\iota_{X}(X)\subset\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)
is dense by
Lemma

A.3
.
Therefore,
\widetilde{f}
is unique if it exists.
For the existence, we denote by
f^{{\mathbin{*}}}\colon\mathrm{C}(Y,\mathbf{R})\to\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})
the natural pullback homomorphism.
Then
\widetilde{f}=\mathrm{MSpec}(f^{{\mathbin{*}}})
does the job (see
Definitions

A.11
and
A.24
).
∎

A.32 Theorem
.

Let
X
be a topological space,
Y
a compact Hausdorff space, and
f\colon X\to Y
a continuous map.
Then there is a unique continuous map
\widetilde{f}\colon\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)\to Y
that makes the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MSpec}\big(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}

commute.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
Theorems

A.30
and
A.31
.
∎

A.33 Corollary
.

Let
X
be a topological space.
Then both

\big(\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{R})),\iota_{X}\big)\text{\hskip 24.00024ptand\hskip 24.00024pt}\big(\mathrm{MSpec}(\mathrm{C}(X,\mathbf{C})),\iota_{X\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}}\big)

are Čech–Stone compactifications of
X
.

Proof.

Combine
Corollaries

A.28
and
A.32
.
∎

Appendix B
A profinite analogue of Quillen’s Theorem B

The goal of this appendix is to prove
Theorem

B.7
, an analogue of Quillen’s Theorem B after completion at a set of primes.
Most of the material here is a part of the sixth author’s thesis
[
Wol25
, §7.3]
.
Nevertheless, here the main result is formulated slightly more generally and the exposition was changed to make it more readable for those less familiar with the theory of internal higher categories developed by the fifth and sixth authors.

B.1
Quillen’s Theorem B

Given a functor of
\infty
-categories
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, Quillen’s Theorem B
[
MR0338129
, Theorem B]
gives a way of calculating the homotopy fiber of the induced map of classifying anima
\mathrm{B}f\colon\mathrm{B}\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{B}\mathcal{D}
.
We begin this appendix by giving a short and model-independent proof of Theorem B that is easier to generalize than Quillen’s original argument.

B.1 Theorem

((Quillen’s Theorem B))
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a functor of
\infty
-categories such that for any
d\to d^{\prime}\smallin\mathcal{D}
the induced map

\mathrm{B}\mathcal{C}_{/d}\to\mathrm{B}\mathcal{C}_{/d^{\prime}}

is an equivalence.
Then for any
d\smallin\mathcal{D}
, the induced commutative square of anima

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{C}_{/d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{*}}\simeq\mathrm{B}\mathcal{D}_{/d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}f}

is cartesian.

The proof rests on the following observation:

B.2 Proposition
.

Let
p\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{D}
be a left fibration with corresponding straightened functor
\tilde{p}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\textbf{{Ani}}
.
If for each map
s\colon d\to d^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}
, the induced map
\tilde{p}(s)
is an equivalence, then for each
d\smallin\mathcal{D}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

is cartesian.

Proof.

By assumption,
\tilde{p}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\textbf{{Ani}}
factors through the unit map
\mathcal{D}\to\mathrm{B}\mathcal{D}
.
Pulling back the universal left fibration, we thus get a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textbf{{Ani}}_{{\mathbin{*}}/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{D}}
Ani
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}}

in which all squares are cartesian.
Note that since left fibrations are conservative and
\mathrm{B}\mathcal{D}
is an anima,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is an anima.
Since
\mathrm{B}\colon\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty}\to\textbf{{Ani}}
is locally cartesian (see
5.3
), by applying
\mathrm{B}
to the middle and left-hand squares, we get another diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

in which all squares are cartesian, completing the proof.
∎

B.3 Remark
.

The assumptions of
Proposition

B.2
are satisfied whenever the left fibration
p
is additionally a right fibration, i.e., a Kan fibration.

We now need to build the correct left fibration to which we can apply
Proposition

B.2
.
For this we need the following.

B.4 Notation
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be an
\infty
-category.
We write
\Cocart(\mathcal{D})\subset\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty,/\mathcal{D}}
for the subcategory with objects cocartesian fibrations
p\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{D}
and morphisms the cocartesian functors.
We write

\LFib(\mathcal{D})\subset\Cocart(\mathcal{D})

for the full subcategory spanned by the left fibrations.
Note that
\LFib(\mathcal{D})
is also a full subcategory of
\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty,/\mathcal{D}}
.

B.5 Recollection
.

For an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D},
the inclusion
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D},\textbf{{Ani}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{D},\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})
admits a left adjoint given by postcomposition with
\mathrm{B}\colon\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty}\to\textbf{{Ani}}
.
Under the straightening-unstraightening equivalence, this corresponds to a left adjoint of the inclusion

\LFib(\mathcal{D})\hookrightarrow\Cocart(\mathcal{D})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Explicitly, this adjoint sends a cocartesian fibration
p\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{D}
to the unique left fibration
L(p)\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{D}
that fits in a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}

,
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(p)}

where the functor
\iota
is initial.
Indeed, such a factorization exists because left fibrations are the right class in the initial-left fibration factorization system, see, e.g.,
[
Mar21
, § 4.1]
.
This also implies that for any left fibration
q\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{D}
, there is a natural equivalence

\mathrm{Map}_{\Cocart(\mathcal{D})}(p,q)\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty,/\mathcal{D}}}(p,q)\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\LFib(\mathcal{D})}(L(p),q)\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Here, left-hand equivalence holds since for left fibrations every edge is cocartesian.
The right-hand equivalence follows from the fact that the left fibrations are the right class of a factorization system
[
HTT
,
Lemma 5.2.8.19
]
.

In order to prove
Theorem

B.1
, we fix some notation regarding oriented fiber products of
\infty
-categories.

B.6 Recollection
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a functor of
\infty
-categories.
We consider the oriented fiber product (also called comma
\infty
-category)
\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}
defined via the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ev_{0},\ev_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}}}

in
\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty}
.
Note that by the universal property of the pullback, the functors
(\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}},f)\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D}
and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathcal{D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{(-)}}

induce a functor
j\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}
.
By
[
HTT
,
Corollary 2.4.7.12
]
, the projection
\pr_{2}\colon\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}
is a cocartesian fibration.
The cocartesian fibration
\pr_{2}
classifies the functor

\mathcal{D}\to\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}\hskip 11.74988ptd\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{/d}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Furthermore,
f
factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{2}}

and
j
admits a right adjoint given by projecting to the first factor.

Proof of
Theorem

B.1
.

We apply the left adjoint
L
of
B.5
to the cocartesian fibration
\pr_{2}\colon\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}
.
Our assumptions precisely say that the resulting left fibration
L(\pr_{2})\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{D}
satisfies the assumptions of
Proposition

B.2
.
Thus we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{C}_{/d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}(\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{*}}\simeq\mathrm{B}\mathcal{D}_{/d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\mathcal{D}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}L(\pr_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

where the outer square is cartesian.
Furthermore, since
\mathrm{B}
inverts adjoints and initial functors (see, e.g.,
[
CJ24
, Corollary 2.11(4) & Remark 2.20]
), the right square is cartesian.
Thus the left square is cartesian, as desired.
∎

B.2
Profinite Theorem B

The goal of this subsection is to prove a variant of Quillen’s Theorem B for profinite categories following the general strategy of
Section

B.1
.
The main ingredient of the proof of
Theorem

B.1
was the straightening-unstraightening equivalence.
However profinite categories are not well-behaved enough to admit a full straightening-unstraightening equivalence.
The solution is to embed profinite categories into condensed categories, where we have a straightening-unstraightening equivalence thanks to
[
Mar22
, Theorem 6.3.1]
.
The precise theorem we aim to prove in this subsection is the following:

B.7 Theorem
.

Let
\Sigma
be a nonempty set of prime numbers.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a map in
\mathrm{Cat}(\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\mathrm{\pi}}))
such that for any map
d\to d^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}
the map of condensed anima

\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathcal{C}_{/d})\to\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathcal{C}_{/d^{\prime}})

becomes an equivalence after
\Sigma
-completion.
Then, for all
d\smallin\mathcal{D}
, the induced map

\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathcal{C}_{/d})\to\textup{fib}_{d}(\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}f)

becomes an equivalence after
\Sigma
-completion.

As mentioned above, straightening-unstraightening plays a crucial role in our proof.
Thus, we begin by defining cocartesian fibrations of condensed
\infty
-categories.

B.8 Definition
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a condensed
\infty
-category.

(1)

A functor
p\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}
of condensed
\infty
-categories is a
cocartesian fibration
if for each
S\smallin\ProFin
, the induced functor
p(S)\colon\mathcal{P}(S)\to\mathcal{C}(S)
is a cocartesian fibration and, furthermore, for each map
\alpha\colon T\to S
in
\ProFin
, the functor
\alpha^{*}\colon\mathcal{P}(S)\to\mathcal{P}(T)
sends
p(S)
-cocartesian morphisms to
p(T)
-cocartesian morphisms.

(2)

A cocartesian fibration
p\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}
is a
left fibration
if for each
S\smallin\ProFin
, the induced functor
p(S)\colon\mathcal{P}(S)\to\mathcal{C}(S)
is a left fibration.

(3)

We write
\Cocart^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{C})
for the subcategory of
\Cond(\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})_{/\mathcal{C}}
with objects the cocartesian fibrations and morphisms the functors
f\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
over
\mathcal{C}
such that for every
S\smallin\ProFin
, the functor
f(S)
preserves cocartesian morphisms.
We write
\LFib^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{C})\subset\Cocart^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{C})
for the full subcategory spanned by the cocartesian fibrations.

B.9 Remark
.

Let us denote by
\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cocart}}([1],\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})
the subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}([1],\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})
with objects cocartesian fibrations and a morphism from
p\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}
to
p^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is a square squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}

such that
f
sends
p
-cocartesian morphisms to
p^{\prime}
-cocartesian morphisms.
Then combining
[
GHN17
, Theorem 4.5]
and
[
HA
,
Proposition 7.3.2.6
]
shows that the inclusion

\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cocart}}([1],\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Fun}([1],\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})

is a right adjoint.
In particular, the inclusion preserves limits.

Let
p\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}
be a functor of condensed
\infty
-categories.
The closure of
\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cocart}}([1],\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})
under limits in
\mathrm{Fun}([1],\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})
shows that if
p
is a cocartesian fibration, then any map of condensed anima
s\colon B\to A
, the functor
s^{{\mathbin{*}}}
in the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(A,\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(B,\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(A,\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(B,\mathcal{C})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{{\mathbin{*}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{{\mathbin{*}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{{\mathbin{*}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{{\mathbin{*}}}}

sends
p(A)
-cocartesian morphisms to
p(B)
-cocartesian morphisms.
Thus, using
[
Mar22
, Proposition 3.17]
, it follows that our definition of cocartesian fibration agrees with the definition given in
[
Mar22
]
in the case
\mathcal{B}=\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})
.

B.10 Remark
.

By
Remark

6.4
, a functor of condensed
\infty
-categories
p\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{C}
is a left fibration in the sense of
Definition

B.8
if and only if
p^{\op}
is a right fibration in the sense of
Definition

6.2
.
Furthermore, if
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{C}
is a left fibration and
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}
is a cocartesian fibration, then every functor
f\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{F}
of condensed
\infty
-categories over
\mathcal{C}
is a map in
\Cocart^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{C})
.

For the condensed version of straighetning-unstraightening, we need to consider the condensed
\infty
-category of condensed
\infty
-categories:

B.11 Definition
.

We write
\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})
for the condensed
\infty
-category given by the assignment

\ProFin^{\op}\smallni S\mapsto\mathrm{Cat}(\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})_{/S})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

B.12 Theorem

((
[
Mar22
, Theorem 6.3.1]
and
[
Mar21
, Theorem 4.5.1]
))
.

There is an natural equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\Cocart^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{C})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{C},\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty}))

Moreover, this equivalence restricts to a natural equivalence

\LFib^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{C})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{C},\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Ani}}))\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

We also have the following analogue of
B.5
for condensed
\infty
-categories:

B.13 Observation
.

Recall that the inclusion
\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})\hookrightarrow\Cond(\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})
admits a left adjoint
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\colon\Cond(\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})\to\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})
.
It is easy to see that both of these functors are compatible with basechange and therefore lift to an adjunction of condensed
\infty
-categories

{\iota}\colon{\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Ani}})}\rightleftarrows{\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})}{\mathchoice{\mathop{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle{\colon}$}}}{\mathop{\reflectbox{$\textstyle{\colon}$}}}{\mathop{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle{\colon}$}}}{\mathop{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\colon}$}}}}{\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}

i.e., an adjunction in the
(\infty,2)
-category of condensed
\infty
-categories.
See also
[
MW24
, Definition 3.1.1 and Proposition 3.2.14]
.
Thus the induced functor

\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{C},\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Ani}}))\to\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{C},\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty}))

admits a left adjoint given by postcomposition with
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}
.

Under the straightening-unstraightening equivalence of
Theorem

B.12
, this corresponds to a left adjoint
L
of the inclusion

\LFib^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{C})\hookrightarrow\Cocart^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{C})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Since left fibrations of condensed categories are the right class in the initial-left fibration factorization systems, as in
B.5
, it follows from
[
HTT
,
Lemma 5.2.8.19
]
that the left adjoint is given by factoring
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}
into an initial functor followed by a left fibration.

To follow the strategy outlined in
Section

B.1
, we need a version of
Proposition

B.2
.
Now another complication enters.
Unlike in
Section

B.1
, the maps
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathcal{C}_{/d})\to\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathcal{C}_{/d^{\prime}})
are not assumed to be equivalences on the nose, but only after
\Sigma
-completion.
Thus, we also need an analogue of
Proposition

B.2
that works up to completion.
We prove the following statement, which is a variant of
[
zbMATH07226705
, Corollary 5.4]
:

B.14 Proposition
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an
\infty
-category with colimits and let
L\colon\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})\to\mathcal{X}
be a colimit-preserving functor.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a condensed
\infty
-category and
p\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{C}
a left fibration of condensed
\infty
-categories corresponding via
Theorem

B.12
to a functor of condensed
\infty
-categories
\tilde{p}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Ani}})
.
Assume that for each profinite set
S
, the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})_{/S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}(S)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}

sends all morphisms to equivalences.
Then for every
d\colon S\to\mathcal{C}
, the induced map

\tilde{p}(d)\colon S\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}\to S\times_{\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\mathcal{C}}\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\mathcal{F}

becomes an equivalence after applying
L
.

B.15 Recollection
.

For the proof of the
Proposition

B.14
, we recall that a functor of condensed
\infty
-categories
f\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{C}
is a
Kan fibration
if it is both a left and right fibration.
Equivalently,
f
is Kan fibration if any of the following equivalent conditions is satisfied:

(1)

For any
S\smallin\ProFin
, the functor
f(S)
is a Kan fibration.

(2)

The functor
f
is right orthogonal to all maps of the form
S\times\{\varepsilon\}\to S\times[n]
, where
S\smallin\ProFin
,
n\smallin\mathbf{N}
, and
\varepsilon\smallin\{0,n\}
.

Indeed, this follows immediately from
Remark

6.4
and
[
Mar21
, Lemma 4.1.2]
.

Proof of
Proposition

B.14
.

We work in the
\infty
-category

\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})_{\mathbf{\Delta}}\coloneq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op},\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}}))

of simplicial objects in
\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})
.
We factor
S\to\mathcal{C}
as
S\xrightarrow{i}T\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{C}

where
i
is contained in the smallest saturated class in
(\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})_{\mathbf{\Delta}})_{/\mathcal{C}}
containing all maps of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\varepsilon\}\times S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]\times S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}

where
n\smallin\mathbf{N}
,
\varepsilon\smallin\{0,n\}
, and
S\smallin\ProFin
, and
f
is right orthogonal to these maps.
It follows from
B.15
that
f
is a Kan fibration.
Since Kan fibrations are levelwise Kan fibrations, it follows from
Remark

B.3
that the natural map

\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(S\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F})\to S\times_{\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\mathcal{C}}\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\mathcal{F}

is an equivalence
Thus it suffices to see that the induced map
S\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}\to T\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}
becomes an equivalence after applying
L\circ\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}
.

We note that, by the universality of colimits in
\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})_{\mathbf{\Delta}}
, the class
\mathcal{M}
of all maps
s\colon A\to B
in
(\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})_{\mathbf{\Delta}})_{/\mathcal{C}}
, that have the property that

\textstyle L\colim_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}}(A\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F})\to L\colim_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}}(B\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F})

is an equivalence is a saturated class in the sense of
[
Mar21
, Definition 2.5.5]
.
To see that
i
is contained in
\mathcal{M}
, it therefore suffices to check this for the maps
\{\varepsilon\}\times S\to[n]\times S
, where
S\smallin\ProFin
and
\varepsilon\smallin\{0,n\}
.
Note that since the pulled back functor
([n]\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}\to[n]\times S
is again a left fibration and the pullback of a final functor along a left fibration is final
[
Mar21
, Proof of Proposition 4.4.7]
, the induced funtor
(\{n\}\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}\to([n]\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}
is final.
In particular,

\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}((\{n\}\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F})\to\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(([n]\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F})

is an equivalence, so
\{n\}\times S\to[n]\times S
is in
\mathcal{M}
.
Furthermore, under this equivalence, the induced map

(\{0\}\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}\to\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(([n]\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F})

is identified with the map
(\{0\}\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}\to(\{n\}\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}
induced by
0\to n
in
[n]
(see
Lemma

B.16
and
Remark

B.17
below).
This map is an
L
-equivalence by assumption.
Therefore,
i
is contained in
\mathcal{M}
, which completes the proof.
∎

B.16 Lemma
.

Let
p\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{C}
be a left fibration of condensed
\infty
-categories with straightened functor
\tilde{p}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Ani}})
.
Then for any morphism
\alpha
in
\mathcal{C}(S)
for some
S\smallin\ProFin
, given by
\alpha\colon[1]\times S\to\mathcal{C}
, the map
\tilde{p}(\alpha)
in
\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})_{/S}
is given by composing

(\{0\}\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}\to\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(([1]\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F})

with the inverse of the equivalence
(\{1\}\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}\similarrightarrow\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(([1]\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F})
.

Proof.

By pulling back along
\alpha
we may assume that
\alpha
is the identity.
Also we have an equivalence

\LFib^{\mathrm{cts}}([1]\times S)\simeq\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cts}}([1]\times S,\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Ani}}))\simeq\mathrm{Fun}([1],\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})_{/S})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Now observe that
\tilde{p}(\alpha)
can be computed as
\ev_{1}(\varepsilon\colon\mathrm{const}\ev_{0}\tilde{p}\to\tilde{p})
, where
\varepsilon
denotes the counit of the adjunction
{\mathrm{const}}\colon{\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})_{/S}}\rightleftarrows{\mathrm{Fun}([1],\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})_{/S})}{\mathchoice{\mathop{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle{\colon}$}}}{\mathop{\reflectbox{$\textstyle{\colon}$}}}{\mathop{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle{\colon}$}}}{\mathop{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\colon}$}}}}{\ev_{0}}
.
Translating to the fibrational perspective via
Theorem

B.12
, we obtain a rectangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1\}\times F_{\{0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{\{0\}}\times_{\{0\}\times S}([1]\times S)\simeq[1]\times F_{\{0\}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\{1\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1\}\times S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]\times S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

and we are done once we see that the composite
F_{\{0\}}\to F_{\{0\}}\times_{\{0\}\times S}([1]\times S)\to F
is identified with the inclusion
F_{\{0\}}\to F
after applying
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}
.
But this is clear, since the two inclusions
\{i\}\times F_{\{0\}}\hookrightarrow[1]\times F_{\{0\}},i=0,1,
are identified after applying
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}
and the composite

\{0\}\times F_{\{0\}}\hookrightarrow[1]\times F_{\{0\}}\to F

yields the inclusion
F_{\{0\}}\to F
by construction.
∎

B.17 Remark
.

In the situation of
Lemma

B.16
, we may more generally consider a map

\alpha\colon[n]\times S\to\mathcal{C}

corresponding to a composable sequence of
n
arrows in
\mathcal{C}(S)
.
Let us denote by
j\colon[1]\to[n]
the map that sends
0
to
0
and
1
to
n
.
We then get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\{0\}\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(([1]\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\{1\}\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\{0\}\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(([n]\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\{n\}\times S)\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}

where the middle vertical map is induced by
j
.
Since left fibrations are
smooth

[
Mar21
, Proposition 4.4.7]
, the right horizontal maps are equivalences and thus also the vertical map in the middle is an equivalence.
It follows that the composite of the lower left map with the inverse of the lower right map is equivalent to
\tilde{p}
applied to the composite of the
n
arrows determined by
\alpha
.

One difference between
Proposition

B.14
and
Theorem

B.7
is that in the former we consider fibers over general profinite sets
S
, while in the latter we only look at fibers over points.
To reduce from profinite sets to points, we use the following observation:

B.18 Lemma
.

Let
\Sigma
be a nonempty set of prime numbers.
Consider a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

in
\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})
such that
A
is the colimit of a diagram
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}\to\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\mathrm{\pi}})\to\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})
and
S,T\smallin\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\Sigma})
.
Then this square remains cartesian after
\Sigma
-completion.

Proof.

Since
\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})
is an
\infty
-topos, geometric realizations are universal in
\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})
.
By
[
Haine:profinite_completions_of_products
, Example 1.9 and Corollary 1.13]
, geometric realizations are also universal in
\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\Sigma})
.
Thus we may assume that
A\smallin\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\mathrm{\pi}})
.
Since the functor
\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\mathrm{\pi}})\to\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})
is fully faithful, the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\mathrm{\pi}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\Sigma})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)_{\Sigma}^{\wedge}}

agrees with the
\Sigma
-completion functor
(-)_{\Sigma}^{\wedge}\colon\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\mathrm{\pi}})\to\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\Sigma})
.
The claim now follows from the fact that
\Sigma
-completion is locally cartesian
[
HHW24a
, Proposition 3.18]
.
∎

B.19
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a functor of condensed
\infty
-categories.
We now consider the condensed
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}
defined via the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cond}}([1],\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ev_{0},\ev_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}}}

as in
B.6
.
By by
[
HTT
,
Corollary 2.4.7.12
]
, the projection
\pr_{2}\colon\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}
is a cocartesian fibration of condensed
\infty
-categories.

For sake of completeness we verify the following two facts which we have already used for ordinary
\infty
-categories in the proof of
Theorem

B.1
.
First recall that by unstraightening the cocartesian fibration of condensed
\infty
-categories
\ev_{1}\colon\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cond}}([1],\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
, one sees that overcategories of condensed
\infty
-categories are functorial.

B.20 Proposition
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a functor of condensed
\infty
-categories and consider the natural cocartesian fibration
\pr_{2}\colon\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}
.
Then for every profinite set
S
and morphism
d\to d^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}(S)
, the induced functor on fibers is the canonical functor

\mathcal{C}_{/d}=\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}_{/d}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}_{/d^{\prime}}=\mathcal{C}_{/d^{\prime}}

in
\Cond(\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})_{/S}
induced by the slice functoriality
\mathcal{D}_{/d}\to\mathcal{D}_{/d^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

We observe that the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cond}}([1],\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ev_{0},\ev_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}}}

is in fact a pullback square in
\Cocart^{\mathrm{cts}}(\mathcal{D})
.
Under the equivalence of
Theorem

B.12
, it therefore corresponds to a cartesian square of functors
\mathcal{D}\to\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{/(-)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{const}(\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{const}(\mathcal{D})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

which proves the claim.
∎

B.21 Lemma
.

For any functor of condensed
\infty
-categories
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, the functor
j\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}
is a fully faithful left adjoint.

Proof.

The functor
j
sits inside the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{cond}}([1],\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{const}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ev_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in which all squares are cartesian.
Since
\mathrm{const}
is the fully faithful left adjoint of
\ev_{0}
, the proof of
[
MW24
, Lemma 6.3.9]
shows that
j
is also a fully faithful left adjoint.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

B.7
.

We factor
f
as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{2}}

and apply the left adjoint of
B.13
to the cocartesian fibration
\pr_{2}
.
The resulting left fibration
p\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{C}
classifies the functor

\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\circ\widetilde{\pr}_{2}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\textbf{{Cond}}(\textbf{{Ani}})

and is given by factoring

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where
\iota
is initial and
p
is a left fibration.
Here,
\widetilde{\pr}_{2}
is the unstraightened functor of
\pr_{2}
.

We now apply
Proposition

B.14
to the left fibration
p
, with
L
the
\Sigma
-completion functor

(-)_{\Sigma}^{\wedge}\colon\Cond(\textbf{{Ani}})\to\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\Sigma})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Thus we have to verify that for any
S\smallin\ProFin
and any map
\alpha\colon d\to d^{\prime}\smallin\mathcal{C}(S)
, the induced map
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\widetilde{\pr}_{2}(\alpha)
becomes an equivalence after
\Sigma
-completion.
By construction
\widetilde{\pr}_{2}(d)
is defined via a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\pr}_{2}(d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

and similarly for
\widetilde{\pr}_{2}(d^{\prime})
.
It follows that both
\widetilde{\pr}_{2}(d)
and
\widetilde{\pr}_{2}(d^{\prime})
are in
\mathrm{Cat}(\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\pi}))
since the latter is closed under limits in
\Cond(\textbf{{Cat}}_{\infty})
.
It follows that for any point
s\colon{\mathbin{*}}\to S
the cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\widetilde{\pr}_{2}(d\circ s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\widetilde{\pr}_{2}(d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

satisfies the assumptions of
Lemma

B.18
, since
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}
is the geometric realization of the corresponding simplicial object.
Thus it remains cartesian after
\Sigma
-completion (also the same holds for
d^{\prime}
instead of
d
).
By
[
SAG
,
Theorem E.3.6.1
]
, equivalences in
\Pro(\textbf{{Ani}}_{\Sigma})
can be checked fiberwise.
Thus we may thus reduce to the case where
S={\mathbin{*}}
.
But in this case
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\widetilde{\pr}_{2}(\alpha)
is by construction the map

\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathcal{C}_{/d})\to\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathcal{C}_{/d^{\prime}})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}

which becomes an equivalence after
\Sigma
-completion by assumption.
Thus,
Proposition

B.14
shows that in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\mathcal{C}_{/d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\vec{\times}}_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{*}}\simeq\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\mathcal{D}_{/d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\mathcal{D}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {,}\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}L(\pr_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

the outer square is cartesian.
Since
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cond}}
inverts left adjoints and initial functors of condensed
\infty
-categories, the claim follows.
∎

Appendix C
Galois groups of function fields

It is well-known that there is an isomorphism of profinite groups

\Freepf_{\mathbf{C}}\simeq\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathbf{C}(T)}

between the free profinite group on the underlying set of
\mathbf{C}
and the absolute Galois group of the function field
\mathbf{C}(T)
.
See
[
Dou64
,
HJ00
]
.
Moreover, it seems to be folklore that this isomorphism can be chosen so that the free profinite group generated by an element
a\smallin\mathbf{C}
corresponds to a decomposition group of the prime
(T-a)
.
See
[
Jar95
, §1.8]
.
The purpose of this appendix is to record a proof of this folklore statement.
This was also implicitly shown in
[
KN71
]
, and we do not claim originality of any of the results in this appendix.

C.1 Notation
.

Throughout this section we fix an algebraic closure
K
of the function field
\mathbf{C}(T)
.
We write
\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathbf{C}(T)}\coloneq\mathrm{Gal}(K/\mathbf{C}(T))
.

C.2 Recollection
.

Write
\overline{\mathbf{C}[T]}\subset K
for the integral closure of
\mathbf{C}[T]
in
K
.
For any
a\smallin\mathbf{C}
a choice of prime ideal
\bar{a}
in
\overline{\mathbf{C}[T]}
lying over
(T-a)
then determines a decomposition group
\mathrm{D}_{\bar{a}}\subset\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathbf{C}(T)}
.
Moreover, if
\bar{a}^{\prime}
is another choice of prime above
(T-a)
, then
\mathrm{D}_{\bar{a}^{\prime}}
is conjugate to
\mathrm{D}_{\bar{a}}
.

Our goal is to prove the following result, which is a slight refinement of
[
Dou64
, Theorem 2]
for
C=\mathbf{C}
.

C.3 Theorem
.

There is an isomorphism of profinite groups

\Freepf_{\mathbf{C}}\to\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathbf{C}(t)}

such that for each
a\smallin\mathbf{C}
the image of
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}(a)
under this isomorphism is the decomposition group
\mathrm{D}_{\bar{a}|a}
of a prime
\bar{a}
lying over
(T-a)
.

C.4 Definition
.

Let
M
be a set.
Write
\Sigma
for the system of finite subsets
S\subset M
partially ordered by inclusion.
Let
((G_{S})_{S\smallin\Sigma},(\rho^{T}_{S})_{S\subset T})
be an inverse system of profinite groups with limit
G_{M}\coloneq\lim_{S\smallin\Sigma}G_{S}
and write
\rho^{M}_{S}\colon G_{M}\to G_{S}
for the canonical projection.
Let
N
be either the whole of
M
, or an element of
\Sigma
.

(1)

We say that a function
\varphi\colon N\to G_{N}
is
adapted
if
\rho^{N}_{S}(\varphi(n))=1
for all finite subsets
S\subset N
and all
n\not\smallin S
.

(2)

We say that a function
\varphi\colon N\to G_{N}
is an
adapted basis
if
\varphi
is adapted and if the map
\Freepf_{N}\to G_{N}
induced by
\varphi
is an isomorphism.

(3)

We say that a system
\mathcal{B}=(\mathcal{B}_{S})_{S\smallin\Sigma}
of sets of functions
\mathcal{B}_{S}\subset\Hom(S,G_{S})
is a
system of adapted bases
if the following conditions hold.

(a)

For each
S\smallin\Sigma
,
\mathcal{B}_{S}\subset\Hom(S,G_{S})=\prod_{S}G_{S}
is a nonempty closed subset consisting of adapted bases.

(b)

For each
S\subset T\smallin\Sigma
, and each
\varphi\smallin\mathcal{B}_{T}
, the restriction
S\subset T\xrightarrow{\varphi}G_{T}\xrightarrow{\rho^{T}_{S}}G_{S}
is an element of
\mathcal{B}_{S}
.

C.5 Proposition
.

Let
M
be a set.
Write
\Sigma
for the poset of finite subsets
S\subset M
partially ordered by inclusion.
Let
((G_{S})_{S\smallin\Sigma},(\rho^{T}_{S})_{S\subset T})
be an inverse system of profinite groups with limit
G_{M}\coloneq\lim_{S\smallin\Sigma}G_{S}
and write
\rho^{M}_{S}\colon G_{M}\to G_{S}
for the canonical projection.
Let
\mathcal{B}
be a system of adapted bases.
If all the transition maps
\rho^{T}_{S}\colon G_{T}\to G_{S}
are surjective, then there exists an adapted basis
M\to G_{M}
such that for each
S\smallin\Sigma
, the restriction

S\subset M\to G_{M}\xrightarrow{\rho^{M}_{S}}G_{S}

is a basis contained in
\mathcal{B}_{S}
.

Proof.

In
[
Dou64
, Theorem 1]
, Douady proved the above claim in the case where
\mathcal{B}
is the system of adapted bases consisting of
\mathcal{B}_{S}
the set of all adapted bases
S\to G_{S}
.
However, the argument he gives actually only uses the axiomatic of a general system of adapted bases in the above sense.
∎

We will use the following lemma:

C.6 Lemma
.

Let
G
be a profinite group and let
H,H^{\prime}\subset G
be closed subgroups.
Let
\alpha\colon G\to G^{\prime}
be a homomorphism of profinite groups.
Let

M\coloneq\{\,g\smallin G\,|\,\alpha(g^{-1})\alpha(H)\alpha(g)=\alpha(H^{\prime})\,\}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Then
M
is closed in
G
.

Proof.

We first consider the set

M^{\prime}\coloneq\{\,g\smallin G\,|\,\alpha(g^{-1})\alpha(H)\alpha(g)\subset\alpha(H^{\prime})\,\}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

For
h\smallin H
, write

M_{h}^{\prime}\coloneq\{\,g\smallin G\,|\,\alpha(g^{-1}hg)\smallin\alpha(H^{\prime})\,\}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

This is preimage of
\alpha(H^{\prime})\subset G^{\prime}
under the continuous map
G\to G^{\prime}
that sends
g
to
\alpha(g^{-1}hg)
.
Since
\alpha(H^{\prime})\subset G^{\prime}
is closed it follows that
M^{\prime}_{h}
is closed.
Since

M^{\prime}=\bigcap_{h\smallin H}M^{\prime}_{h}

it follows that
M^{\prime}
is closed.
Now note that the same argument shows that

M^{\prime\prime}\coloneq\{\,g\smallin G\,|\,\alpha(g)\alpha(H^{\prime})\alpha(g)^{-1}\subset\alpha(H)\,\}

is closed.
Thus
M=M^{\prime}\cap M^{\prime\prime}
is closed.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

C.3
.

Our choice of algebraic closure yields an isomorphism

\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathbf{C}(T)}\similarrightarrow\lim_{S\subset\mathbf{C}\textup{ finite}}\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(\mathbf{A}^{1}\smallsetminus S,\bar{\eta})\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

Let us write
G_{S}=\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}(\mathbf{A}^{1}\smallsetminus{S},\bar{\eta})
.
We want to apply
Proposition

C.5
to this inverse systems of groups and the system of adapted bases
\mathcal{B}_{S}
that consists of those maps
\varphi\colon S\to G_{S}
that are adapted bases and for any
s\smallin S
, the subgroup
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}(\varphi(s))
is (conjugate to) a decomposition group at
s
.
To see that
(\mathcal{B}_{S})_{S}
is a system of adapted bases, we need to show that the conditions
Definition

C.4
-(
3
.
3a
)
and
Definition

C.4
-(
3
.
3b
)
are satisfied.
It is clear that (
3
.
3b
) is satisfied, so we only check (
3
.
3a
).
We start by verifying that
\mathcal{B}_{S}\subset\Hom(S,G_{S})
is closed.
To this end, note that the larger subset
\mathcal{B}^{\text{all}}_{S}\subset\Hom(S,G_{S})
, consisting of all adapted bases is closed, see the beginning of the proof of
[
Sza09
, Proposition 3.4.9]
.
To conclude, it suffices to see that for all
s\smallin S
the subset
\Sigma_{s}\subset G_{S}
, consisting of those
\sigma\smallin G_{S}
with the property that
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}(\sigma)
is a decomposition group at
s
, is closed.
Indeed, in this case

\mathcal{B}_{S}=\mathcal{B}^{\text{all}}_{S}\cap\prod_{s\smallin S}\Sigma_{s}\subset\Hom(S,G_{S})=\prod_{S}G_{S}\hbox to0.0pt{\ {.}\hss}

is seen to be an intersection of closed subsets, hence itself closed.
Fix one decomposition group
\mathrm{D}_{s}
at
s
.
Since
\mathrm{D}_{s}\simeq\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}
, the subset
N\subset\mathrm{D}_{s}
of elements that topologically generate
\mathrm{D}_{s}
is closed.
Now observe that
\Sigma_{s}
agrees with the image of the continuous map

N\times G_{S}\to G_{S};\;\;(n,g)\mapsto g^{-1}ng

and is therefore closed, since the domain is compact.
Finally, we need to check that
\mathcal{B}_{S}\neq\varnothing
.
Choose a point
x\smallin\mathbf{C}\smallsetminus S
and an étale path
\alpha\colon\bar{\eta}\rightsquigarrow x
and consider the isomorphism

\psi\colon\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathbf{C}\smallsetminus S,x)^{\wedge}{}\similarrightarrow{\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}\smallsetminus{S},x){}\similarrightarrow{\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\et}}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}\smallsetminus{S},\bar{\eta})

obtained from the Riemann existence theorem and conjugation with
\alpha^{-1}
.
Recall that
\mathrm{\pi}_{1}^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathbf{C}\smallsetminus S,x)
is freely generated by simple loops
\gamma_{s}
at
x
around
s
, that do not loop around other points in
S
.
Then
(s\mapsto\psi(\gamma_{s}))
is clearly an adapted basis and furthermore
\psi(\gamma_{s})
generates a decomposition group at
s
.
Thus
(s\mapsto\psi(\gamma_{s}))\smallin\mathcal{B}_{S}
.

By applying
Proposition

C.5
, we obtain an isomorphism
\varphi\colon\Freepf_{\mathbf{C}}{}\similarrightarrow{\mathrm{Gal}}_{\mathbf{C}(T)}
with the property that for all finite subsets
S\subset\mathbf{C}
and
a\smallin S
,
(\rho^{\mathbf{C}}_{S}\circ\varphi)(a)
generates a decomposition group at
a
in
G_{S}
.
We now show that
\varphi(a)
generates a decomposition group at
a
in
\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathbf{C}(T)}
for any
a\smallin\mathbf{C}
.
To this end, fix one decomposition group
\mathrm{D}_{a}\subset\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathbf{C}(T)}
of
a
.
By the above, for every finite subset
S\subset\mathbf{C}
there exists some
g\smallin\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathbf{C}(T)}
such that
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}(\varphi(a))=g^{-1}\mathrm{D}_{a}g
in
G_{S}
.
Now by
Lemma

C.6
the set
C_{S}
of all such
g
is closed.
Therefore
\bigcap_{S}C_{S}=\lim_{S}C_{S}
is nonempty as a cofiltered limit of nonempty compact Hausdorff spaces.
By construction, any element
g\smallin\bigcap_{S}C_{S}
has the property that
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}(\varphi(a))=g^{-1}\mathrm{D}_{a}g
holds after projecting to
G_{S}
simultaneously for all
S\subset\mathbf{C}
finite.
Since both
\mathrm{D}_{a}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}(\varphi(a))
are closed subgroups of
\mathrm{Gal}_{\mathbf{C}(T)}=\lim_{S\subset\mathbf{C}\text{ finite}}G_{S}
, this shows that indeed
g^{-1}\mathrm{D}_{a}g=\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}(\varphi(a))
.
In particular,
\varphi(a)
generates a decomposition group as desired.
∎
